Exploring the Structure of Higher Algebroids
Mikołaj Rotkiewicz

Abstract
The notion of a higher-order algebroid , as introduced by Jóźwikowski and Rotkiewicz in their work Higher-order analogs of Lie algebroids via vector bundle comorphisms (SIGMA, 2018), generalizes the concepts of a higher-order tangent bundle τ M k : T k ​ M → M \tau^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\to M and a (Lie) algebroid. This idea is based on a (vector bundle) comorphism approach to (Lie) algebroids and the reduction procedure of homotopies from the level of Lie groupoids to that of Lie algebroids. In brief, an alternative description of a Lie algebroid ( A , [ ⋅ , ⋅ ] , ♯ ) (A,[\cdot,\cdot],\sharp) is a vector bundle comorphism κ \kappa , defined as the dual of the Poisson map ε : T ∗ ​ A → T ​ A ∗ \varepsilon:\mathrm{T}^{\ast}A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}A^{\ast} associated with the Lie algebroid A A . The framework of comorphisms has proven to be a suitable language for describing higher-order analogues of Lie algebroids from the perspective of the role played by (Lie) algebroids in geometric mechanics. In this work, we uncover the classical algebraic structures underlying the somewhat mysterious description of higher-order algebroids through comorphisms. For the case k = 2 k=2 , we establish a one-to-one correspondence between higher-order Lie algebroids and pairs consisting of a two-term representation (up to homotopy) of a Lie algebroid and a morphism to the adjoint representation of this algebroid.



This research was supported by the Polish National Science Center under the grant DEC-2012/06/A/ST1/00256.

MSC 2020:

58A20, 58A50, 17B66, 17B70

Keywords:

higher algebroids, representations up to homotopy, graded manifolds, graded bundles, VB-algebroids, Lie algebroids

1
Introduction

In
[
JR18
]
the notion of a higher algebroid was introduced,
based on extensive studies of examples we would like to refer to as higher algebroids (HAs, for short)
[
JR13
,
JR15
]
.
Our intuitive thinking was that a higher algebroid should represent a geometric and algebraic structure that generalizes higher-order tangent bundles in a similar manner as algebroids generalize tangent bundles.
In the first order, the algebroid structure is defined on a vector bundle (VB, for short), with the most obvious example being the tangent bundle
\tau_{M}:\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow M
. In higher orders,
\tau_{M}
is replaced by the higher-order tangent bundle
\tau^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
, which for
k>1
is no longer a vector bundle but a

graded bundle
, in the terminology introduced in
[
GR11
]
.

It is referred to as an
\mathbb{N}
-graded manifold in
[
JR18
]
.

In a graded bundle there exists a distinguished class of
graded fiber coordinates
, taking over linear coordinates, with transition functions represented as homogeneous polynomials. In a particular case of polynomials of degree one (linear maps), one gets vector bundles as a special case.
From various perspectives discussed in
[
JR18
]
, it became apparent that the structure of a higher algebroid should be defined on a graded bundle.

The most common way to motivate the concept of a Lie algebroid comes from the reduction of the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
. As a geometric object, the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}
is the set
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G}):=\mathrm{T}_{M}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
, consisting of tangent vectors in the direction of the source fibration
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
of
\mathcal{G}
and based at
M
– the base of
\mathcal{G}
.
The structure of the tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
, induces a certain structure on
\mathrm{T}_{M}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
, leading to the notion of a Lie algebroid.
Typically, the structure of a Lie algebroid is expressed by means of a bracket operation
[\cdot,\cdot]
on the space of sections of a vector bundle
\sigma:A\rightarrow M
and a VB morphism
\sharp:A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
called
the anchor map
. However, it is obvious that this approach has no direct generalization to higher-order case, because there is no bracket operation on the space of sections of
\tau^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
, since
\tau^{k}_{M}
is not a vector bundle for
k>1
and, in particular, its sections cannot be added.

In light of the above, it is natural to consider the reduction
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{M}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
of the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{G}
as a prototype of a higher-order algebroid of order
k
. The reduction map
\mathcal{R}^{k}:\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G}):=\mathrm{T}^{k}_{M}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
takes a
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-velocity
[g]_{k}
represented by a curve
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, lying in a single fiber of the foliation
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
, to the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-velocity based at a point in
M
. A natural problem arises: how to characterize the structure on
\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G})
inherited from the groupoid multiplication. In
[
JR15
]
we proposed an answer to this question by reducing the natural map
\kappa^{k}_{\mathcal{G}}:\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{G}
.

In the first order, one reduces the canonical involution
\kappa_{\mathcal{G}}
which results in a relation
\kappa\subset\mathrm{T}\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})\times\mathrm{T}\mathcal{A}(G)
and leads to an alternative definition of the structure of a Lie algebroid as a pair
(A,\kappa)
consisting of a vector bundle
\sigma:A\rightarrow M
and a relation
\kappa\subset\mathrm{T}A\times\mathrm{T}A
of a special kind . This viewpoint on Lie algebroids was first introduced in
[
GU99
]
.
It turns out that
\kappa
is the dual of the Poisson map
\varepsilon:\mathrm{T}^{\ast}A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}A^{\ast}
associated with the linear Poisson tensor on
A^{\ast}
.
1
1

1

Linear Poisson structures on the dual bundle
A^{\ast}
are in a one-to-one correspondence with Lie algebroid structures on
A
.
As
\varepsilon
is a VB morphism, the dual
\kappa=\varepsilon^{\ast}
is a VB comorphism, see Definition
2.4
.
The comorphism approach to (Lie) algebroids is also very natural from the perspective of variational calculus. The relation
\kappa
was recognized as a ’tool’ in constructing admissible variations in geometric mechanics
[
GG08
]
.

Based on the properties of the reduction of
\kappa^{k}_{\mathcal{G}}
and its potential applications in variational calculus, we introduced higher algebroids in
[
JR18
]
as pairs
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
consisting of a graded bundle
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
of order
k
, equipped with a vector bundle comorphism
\kappa^{k}
. This comorphism relates the vector bundles
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
and
\mathrm{T}E^{k}\rightarrow E^{k}
and satisfies certain natural axioms.
2
2

2

In the comorphism approach, it is natural to consider generalizations of the notion of a Lie algebroid obtained by relaxing its axioms. In the literature on geometric mechanics, these generalizations are known as ’almost Lie’ algebroids (where the Jacobi identity is not assumed), ’skew’ algebroids (where neither the Jacobi identity nor the anchor-bracket compatibility is required), and ’general’ algebroids (where, in addition, the skew-symmetry of the bracket is not required).
We recall from
[
JR18
]
a detailed formulation of these axioms in Definition
2.7
.

The definition of HAs given in
[
JR18
]
, which we consider to be very natural from many perspectives, also appears to be quite mysterious. The goal of the present work was to unveil the vector bundle morphisms, brackets, and other operations hidden within the comorphism description of HAs. A complete solution is achieved in the case of
k=2
.

Our solution situates Lie HAs within the realm of
representations up to homotopy
(representations u.t.h., in short) of Lie algebroids, the concept introduced in
[
AC12
]
. The idea is to represent Lie algebroids using cochain complexes of vector bundles. Such a complex is given ’an action’ of a Lie algebroid represented by an
A
-connection which is flat only ’up to homotopy’ governed by higher order homotopy operators. When the complex consists of only one term, this notion reduces to a genuine representation of a Lie algebroid on a vector bundle. An important example for us is the notion of the
adjoint representation
whose proper generalization from the field of Lie algebras to that of Lie algebroids is found within the framework of representations up to homotopy.
As explained in
[
AC12
]
, the adjoint representation of a Lie algebroid
A
is manifested by ’an action up to homotopy’ on the two-term complex
\sharp:A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
, where
\sharp
is the anchor map. On the other hand, it was found in
[
GSM10
]
that 2-term representations u.t.h. have an elegant description by means of VB-algebroids — Lie algebroid objects in the category of vector bundles. It this correspondence the adjoint representation of a Lie algebroid
A
is nothing more but the VB-algebroid structure on
\mathrm{T}A
– the tangent prolongation of the Lie algebroid
A
. Our solution also recognizes this point of view.

Our results.

The main result is presented in Theorem
3.26
and Corollary
3.27
where we establish a one-to-one correspondence between higher algebroids of order two and morphisms between representations u.t.h. of Lie algebroids of a specific nature as presented in the diagram:

Order-two Lie higher algebroids

(E^{2},\kappa^{2})

one-to-one
correspondence

Representations u.t.h. of a Lie algebroid
A

on a two-term complex
A\rightarrow C

together with a morphism
\Phi
(of a special form)

to the adjoint representation
\operatorname{ad}_{\nabla}
of
A

where
\nabla
is a fixed linear connection on a vector bundle
A\rightarrow M
.
On the left is a Lie HA structure defined on a graded bundle
E^{2}\rightarrow M
characterised by a special type of relation denoted as
\kappa^{2}
, which is a subset of
\mathrm{T}^{2}E^{1}\times_{M}\mathrm{T}E^{2}
. In this correspondence,
A=E^{1}
is the reduction of the graded bundle
E^{2}
to degree
1
, with the Lie algebroid structure inherited from
\kappa^{2}
. Furthermore, the vector bundle
C\to M
is introduced as the
core
of
E^{2}
, as explained in Section
2
.
On the right-hand side, we have a representation u.t.h. of the Lie algebroid
A
defined on a two-term cochain complex
A\rightarrow C
. Additionally, there is a morphism denoted as
\Phi
that connects this representation to the adjoint representation
\operatorname{ad}_{\nabla}
of
A
in the sense of
[
AC12
, Definition 3.3]
, and further,
\Phi
is of special type: the 1-form component of
\Phi
vanishes and so
\Phi
is a map of cochain complexes and, moreover,
\Phi
is the identity on
A
in degree 0.

Following the ideas of
[
GSM10
,
DJLO15
]
, we found that such a morphism
\Phi
corresponds to a VB-algebroid morphism to the adjoint representation of
A
represented as the VB-algebroid
\mathrm{T}A
(see Corollary
3.27
). This construction makes the choice of a linear connection
\nabla
unnecessary. In summary, order-two Lie HAs are characterised by VB-algebroid morphisms
\Psi:D\to\mathrm{T}A
from a VB-algebroid
D
to the tangent prolongation of a Lie algebroid
A
, such that
\Psi
is the identity on the underlying algebroid
A
, and on the core bundle, which is also identified with the vector bundle
A
.

These results are obtained in a few steps which we discuss below.

Map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
.

In any order
k
, we discover a canonical morphism of graded bundles  denoted by
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}:A^{[k]}\rightarrow E^{k}
(see Definition
3.1
), which is associated with any almost Lie
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order algebroid
3
3

3

In the case when
k=2
, the existence of a morphism
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
is already guaranteed by the weaker assumption that
A
is a skew algebroid.

(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
. Here,
A
is the almost Lie algebroid (AL algebroid, for short)
(E^{1},\kappa^{1})
obtained from
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
by means of the reduction to order one, and
(A^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]})
is the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order prolongation of
A
– a graded bundle with the HA structure naturally induced from the AL algebroid structure on
A
(see (
2.34
) and (
2.36
)). The existence of this map is of crucial importance as it allows to relate properties of an abstract higher algebroid with much better recognized HA
(A^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]})
studied in details in
[
JR15
]
. We recall that if
A=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})
is the Lie algebroid of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
then
A^{[k]}=\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G})=\mathrm{T}^{k}_{M}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
is the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order HA of
\mathcal{G}
. We conjecture (in Conjecture
3.5
) that if
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
is a Lie HA then the structure map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}:A^{[k]}\rightarrow E^{k}
is a morphism of HAs. We were able to prove this in the case
k=2
.

The structure of the graded bundle of a HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2}
).

In general, a graded bundle of order two is obtained from its components: the vector bundle
E^{1}
(the order-one reduction of
E^{2}
), and its core vector bundle, denoted by
\widehat{E^{2}}
, by gluing transition functions that are homogeneous polynomials of degree 2. In what follows,
the vector bundles
E^{1}
and
\widehat{E^{2}}
, are denoted by
A
and
C
, respectively.

With the help of the map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
we can recover the graded bundle
E^{2}\to M
as the quotient of the graded bundle
A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}
, see Lemma
3.6
. Here,
A^{[2]}
is the second-order prolongation of
A
and
C_{[2]}
denotes the graded bundle of order 2 obtained from the vector bundle
C
by assigning weight two to the linear functions on
C
.

Structure maps of HAs.

By focusing solely on the graded bundle structure of
\kappa^{2}
we encounter equations (
2.21
). Our objective is to attribute a geometrical interpretation of the local structure functions
Q^{a}_{i}
,
Q^{a}_{ij}
,
Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}
, etc. present in (
2.21
). It turned out that the functions
Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}
do not correspond to any geometric object, highlighting that such an interpretation is not always straightforward.
However, when we combine
Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}
with
Q^{k}_{ij}
there emerges a three-argument operation, denoted by
\delta
, on the space
\Gamma(A)
of sections of the vector bundle
A
with values in
\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
, where
C
is the core of
E^{2}
, see (
3.20
) and (
3.22
).
The meaning of the other structure functions
4
4

4

We will use the symbol
Q^{\cdots}_{\cdots}
to refer to the structure functions
Q^{a}_{i}
,
Q^{a}_{ij}
, etc. present in (
2.21
).

Q^{\cdots}_{\cdots}
proved to be more straightforward. These include:

(i)

a skew-symmetric bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
on
\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
and a VB morphism
\sharp:A\to\mathrm{T}M
defining a skew algebroid structure on
A
,

(ii)

a morphism of graded bundles (the second-order anchor map)
\sharp^{2}:E^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}M
, which is the base of the comorphism
\kappa^{2}
,

(iii)

a vector bundle morphisms
\partial:A\rightarrow C
,

(iv)

a map
\Box:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
,

(v)

a skew symmetric map
\beta:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
.

There is also another interesting structure map
\psi:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
(see (
3.33
)), which becomes relevant when studying tensorial properties of the aforementioned structure maps. Moreover, the symmetric part of
\psi
, denoted by
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}
, together with the VB morphisms
\sharp^{1}=\sharp
and
\widehat{\sharp^{2}}
(the core of
\sharp^{2}
), allows us to recover the second-order anchor map
\sharp^{2}
, see Lemma
3.12
and Theorem
3.13
. This resolves the problem of presenting axioms of a skew order-two HA entirely in terms of VB morphisms and VB differential operators.

Definitions of all these structure maps are given in Subsection
3.2
. Most of them are obtained through algebroid lifts
\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}(E^{2})
,
s\mapsto{s}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}
, associated with the HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
and Lie brackets of vector fields on
E^{2}
. The definition of algebroid lifts, as seen in (
2.29
), relies on the characteristic property of a VB comorphism: unlike a typical VB morphism, it induces a map between the spaces of sections.

We also introduce a structure map
\omega:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
,
being some modifications of the map
\delta
, see (
3.25
). While it carries the same information as
\delta
, it has better algebraic properties and helps to formulate our results more concisely.
Additionally, we define maps
\xi
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
in Definition
3.10
, which appear in the Leibniz-type formulas for above-mentioned structure maps (Theorem
3.13
). The vanishing of these maps is also included as an axiom of AL HAs or Lie HAs, see Theorems
3.16
and
3.20
. Moreover, for greater precision in formulating certain results, we found it useful to decompose some of these maps, such as
\delta
,
\omega
, and
\psi
, into their symmetric and anti-symmetric parts.

Most of the structure maps mentioned above are multi-differential operators on certain vector bundles. We provide a detailed description of the tensorial properties of
these structure maps and prove that a system of such maps allows to reconstruct the skew HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
, see Theorem
3.13
. This approach can also be extended to
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order HAs for

k>2
, as discussed in Remark
3.9
.

In the next step, we characterize the axioms of an almost Lie HA (Theorem
3.16
) and Lie HA (Theorem
3.20
) in terms of the above mentioned structure maps. In other words, we formulate necessary and sufficient conditions that the structure maps
\beta
,
\square
,
\omega
etc. should satisfy for the related HAs to be, respectively, almost Lie and Lie. Throughout our analysis, we heavily rely on Theorem
2.11
, which provides characterizations of AL and Lie axioms for higher algebroids through algebroid lifts.

HAs over a point.

In case when the base
M
is a point we find a complete description of order-two skew and Lie higher algebroids, see Theorem
3.15
: An order-two skew HA over a point has to split, meaning
E^{2}=\mathfrak{g}_{[1]}\times C_{[2]}
where
\mathfrak{g}=E^{1}
and
C=\widehat{E^{2}}
. Furthermore, in the Lie case, there is a one-to-one correspondence between such Lie HAs and Lie module morphisms
\partial:{\mathfrak{g}}\to{C}
from the adjoint module of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
to the
\mathfrak{g}
-module
C
.

Main result.

The reformulated axioms of Lie HAs and the description of order-two HAs over a point by means of representations of Lie algebras may suggest a relation between HAs and representations of Lie algebroids. Note however that there is no concept of the adjoint representation within the framework of representations on VBs.
It is the setting of representations u.t.h. of Lie algebroids in which the correct generalization of the concept of the adjoint representation of a Lie algebra is possible.
The construction of a Lie algebroid representation out of a Lie HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})

imitates the construction of the adjoint representation given in
[
AC12
]
. It is obtained by means of the structure maps of a Lie HA mentioned earlier. There exist also an obvious map
\Phi
between the complexes
A\to C
and
A\to\mathrm{T}M
which, thanks to the properties of the structure maps of a Lie HA, turns out to be a morphism to the adjoint representation of
A
.
Conversely, if a representation u.t.h. of
A
on a two-term complex of the form
A\to C
is given, and a morphism of complexes
\Phi:(A\to C)\rightarrow(A\to\mathrm{T}M)
is given that also serves as a morphism of representations, then we can extract the structure maps from it and construct a skew HA structure on the graded bundle described in Lemma
3.6
. It can be then verified that these maps satisfy the axioms
of AL and Lie HA given in Theorems
3.16
and
3.20
.

Examples.

Given a Lie algebroid
A
, there are two natural morphisms to the adjoint representation of a Lie algebroid
A
. One is the identity morphism
\Phi
on the adjoint representation. The other one is obtained from
the double of a vector bundle
, described in
[
AC12
]
, which is a representation of the Lie algebroid
A
on a 2-term complex of the form
E\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}}E
. We illustrate HAs corresponding to these two cases in Examples
3.30
and
3.29
, respectively.

Organization of the paper.

Section
2
begins by collecting notations and fundamental constructions
concerning graded bundles, double vector bundles and VB-algebroids. We also introduce a functor, denoted by
\lambda
, which is a generalization of the linearisation functor discovered in
[
BGG16
]
and which is used in the definition of the morphism
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
(Definition
3.1
). We recall also basic definitions from
[
JR18
,
JR15
]
(VB comorphism, higher-order algebroid, prolongations of AL algebroids) and give a definition of algebroid lifts in a slightly different way than in
[
JR18
, Definition 4.8]
, more convenient for computations which we perform in Section
4
. Theorem
2.11
extends Proposition 4.9 from
[
JR18
]
to the AL case. In Lemma
2.14
we express the compatibility of algebroid lifts obtained by means of HAs
\kappa^{k}
and the reduction of
\kappa^{k}
to a lower weight. We also list a few canonical inclusions used in the paper and describe their relationships.

Section
3
is devoted to a detailed analysis of mathematical structures standing behind a comorphism
\kappa^{k}
that defines a HA structure. W begin with the definition and properties of the map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
, which connects an arbitrary HA

(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
with the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order prolongation of its first-order reduction
(E^{1},\kappa^{1})
. We provide coordinate formulas for
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
and
\mathrm{\Theta}^{3}
, see Example
3.3
.

From this point on, we focus solely on the case

k=2
. In Lemma
3.6
we find an explicit construction of a graded bundle
E^{2}\to M
which hosts an order-two HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
. Subsequently,
we introduce several canonical maps associated with this HA referring to them as
"the structure maps of
(E^{2},\kappa^{k})
"
. Most of these maps are differential operators defined on (the product of) the spaces of sections
\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
or
\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
with values in
\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
. The term
"structure functions"
is reserved to functions
Q^{a}_{i}
,
Q^{a}_{ij}
, etc. which are given in Example
2.8
as a local representation of a general order-two HA. These functions depend on the chosen coordinate system on the graded bundle
E^{2}
. Although we work in the case
k=2
we present analogs of the structure maps in any order, see Remark
3.9
.
In Theorem
3.13
we provide an equivalent description of skew, order-two HAs
in terms of the aforementioned structure maps. In Theorem
3.15
we discuss the special case when the base
M
of
E^{2}
is a single point (an order-two analog of a (Lie) algebra) and give a characterization of such structures. It turns out that the Lie condition is very rigorous and all such Lie HAs correspond to morphisms
\partial:C\to A
of Lie algebra modules, where
A
represents the adjoint module of the Lie algebra
A
.

We subsequently examine the conditions in Definition
2.7
characterizing AL and Lie HA and translate them to the level of the structure maps, see Theorem
3.16
and Theorem
3.20
.
Moving forward, in Lemma
3.25
we recognize that data describing order-two Lie HAs gives rise to a representation u.t.h. of the Lie algebroid
A
on the structure map
\partial:A\to C
considered as a two-term complex of vector bundles and also induces a morphism
\Phi
to the adjoint representation of
A
. Remarkably, this data is also sufficient for recovering a HA structure
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
, as demonstrated in Theorem
3.26
. Furthermore, we formulate VB-algebroid version of this correspondence in Corollary
3.27
and illustrate the obtained relationship in Examples
3.30
and
3.29
. We also briefly recall the correspondence between representations u.t.h. and VB-algebroids —- providing the necessary facts on this subject to demonstrate our results.

In Appendix
4
we give proofs for various results, including part (a) of Theorem
3.13
, Theorem
3.15
, Conjecture
3.5
in the case
k=2
and complete the proof of Theorem
3.16
, where more detailed calculations, including those in coordinates, are carried out. Some of these calculations are supported by additional lemmas. One can also find there a brief recollection on representations up to homotopy, guided by
[
AC12
]
. For more in-depth information, interested readers should refer to the existing literature
[
AC12
,
GSM10
,
BGV18
,
GSJLMM18
]
.

Historical remarks.

The studies on HAs, as understood in this paper, were initiated by M. Jóźwikowski and the author of present manuscript in
[
JR13
]
, and continued in
[
JR15
,
JR18
]
. Prior to this, higher-order analogues of Lie algebroids was the subject of
[
Vor10
]
by T. Voronov who proposed that such analogues should be
Q
-manifolds of spacial kind generalizing Vaintrob approach to Lie algebroids
[
Vai97
]
. The most recent studies are due to A. Bruce, K. Grabowska and J. Grabowski
[
BGG16
]
whose idea was to imitates the canonical inclusion
\mathrm{T}^{k}M\subset\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
on the abstract level of graded bundles  having
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
as a prominent example of
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order analogue of a Lie algebroid. As we pointed in
[
JR18
]
, all these approaches lead to different mathematical objects. This distinctiveness is further evident from the classification of order-two (Lie) HAs given in this work.

Acknowledgments.

The author is grateful to Michał Jóźwikowski for his insightful comments on the organization and editing of this work, and for many fruitful discussions regarding the research.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Graded bundles

We shall review basic constructions associated with
graded bundles
that will be used in the present work. For further details, we refer to
[
GR11
]
and additional works
[
BGR16
,
JR18
,
Vor02
]
.

A fundamental example of a graded bundle is the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order tangent bundle
\tau^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
. The elements of
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
are
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order tangency classes
[\gamma]_{k}
of curves
\gamma
in
M
.
5
5

5

[\gamma]_{k}
is also called the
k
-velocity
represented by the curve
\gamma
Then
\mathrm{T}^{1}M=\mathrm{T}M
is the tangent bundle of
M
but for
k>1
,
\tau^{k}_{M}
is not a vector bundle; however, the fibers are still equipped with a special structure, namely, a natural action of the monoid of real numbers
(\mathbb{R},\cdot)
,

h:\mathbb{R}\times\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M,\hskip 9.24994pt(s,[\gamma]_{k})\mapsto[\gamma_{s}]_{k}

where
\gamma_{s}(t)=\gamma(st)
.
Thus, in the terminology of
[
GR11
]
,
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
is a
homogeneity structure
, i.e., a manifold equipped with a smooth action of
(\mathbb{R},\cdot)
.
On the other hand, local coordinates
(x^{a})
on
M
induce
adapted coordinates

6
6

6

f^{({\alpha})}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(T^{k}M)
, given by
f^{({\alpha})}([\gamma]_{k})=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}_{t=0}f(\gamma(t))
, denotes the
({\alpha})
-lift of
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, see
[
Mor70
]
. Hence,

x^{a,({\alpha})}=(x^{a})^{({\alpha})}
denotes the
({\alpha})
-lift of the coordinate function
x^{a}
.

(x^{a,({\alpha})})_{0\leq{\alpha}\leq k}
on
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
which are naturally graded by numbers
0,1,\ldots,k
. On
T^{2}M
they transform as

x^{a^{\prime}}=x^{a^{\prime}}(x),\hskip 9.24994pt\dot{x}^{a^{\prime}}=\frac{\partial x^{a^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{b}}\,\dot{x}^{b},\hskip 9.24994pt\ddot{x}^{a^{\prime}}=\frac{\partial x^{a^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{b}}\,\ddot{x}^{b}+\frac{\partial^{2}x^{a^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{b}\partial x^{c}}\dot{x}^{b}\dot{x}^{c},

where
\dot{x}^{a}=(x^{a})^{(1)}
,
\ddot{x}^{a}=(x^{a})^{(2)}
.
In general, the gradation of coordinates leads to the concept of a graded bundle  i.e., a smooth fiber bundle
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
in which we are given a distinguished class of fiber coordinates, called graded coordinates. Each graded coordinate is assigned its
weight
and transition functions preserve this gradation. An important assumption is made that weights are non-negative integers. (The index
k
in
E^{k}
indicates that all weights are
\leq k
, in which case we say that the graded bundle
\sigma^{k}
is of
order

k
. Graded bundles of order
1
are nothing more than vector bundles.)

It has been shown that both the concept of a homogeneity structure and a graded bundle are equivalent
[
GR11
]
. A graded bundle associated with a homogeneity structure
(E,h)
can be conveniently encoded by means of the
weight vector field
defined as

\Delta(p)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big|_{t=1}h_{t}(p)
. In graded coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i}_{w})

7
7

7

This notation means that
(x^{a})
are functions defined (locally) on the base
M
of the graded bundle
h_{0}:E\rightarrow M
while
(y^{i}_{w})
are fiber coordinates in this bundle. Moreover, the (abundant) notation
y^{i}=y^{i}_{w}
indicates that the function
y^{i}
has weight
w
, i.e., is a homogeneous function (with respect to
h
) of weight
w
.
we have
\Delta=\sum_{i}w^{i}y^{i}_{w}\partial_{y^{i}_{w}}
. A morphism
f
between graded bundles
E
and
F
, colloquially described as a map preserving the gradation of coordinates, can be given a short, precise meaning as a smooth map
f:E\rightarrow F
such that the corresponding weight vector fields,
\Delta_{E}
and
\Delta_{F}
, are
f
-related. Equivalently, this can be described as a smooth map intertwining the corresponding homogeneity structures, i.e.,
f\circ h_{t}^{E}=h_{t}^{F}\circ f
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, where
h_{t}=h(t,\cdot)
.

In this work, we frequently encounter multi-graded structures like
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M
,
\mathrm{T}E^{k}
(the tangent bundle of a graded bundle of order
k
) or
\mathrm{T}^{k}E
(
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order tangent bundle of
E
, where
\sigma:E\rightarrow M
is a vector bundle). In all these examples, there are present two (compatible) graded bundle structures. Such structures can be described as
(F;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})
– a manifold
F
equipped with two weight vector fields
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
, and
the condition of compatibility can be expressed as
[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}]=0
. Equivalently, the last condition can be stated as
h_{t}^{1}\circ h_{s}^{2}=h_{s}^{2}\circ h_{t}^{1}
for any
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
, where
(F,h^{i})
are the homogeneity structures with weight vector field
\Delta_{i}
, for
i=1,2
. Moreover, the bases of the graded bundles
(F,\Delta_{i})
, where
i=1,2
, carry induced graded bundle structures. In this paper, we shall mostly encounter the case when one of these graded bundle structures has order 1 (like in
\mathrm{T}E^{k}
ot
T^{k}E
) and will refer to them as
weighted vector bundles.
8
8

8

Weighted structures, e.g. weighted algebroids, where intensively studied in
[
BGG15a
,
BGG16
]
.
They can be presented as a diagram like

\textstyle{F^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{k}}
\textstyle{F^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{0}}
\textstyle{\underline{F}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\sigma}^{k}}
\textstyle{\underline{F}^{0}.}

(2.1)

where
k
indicates the order of the graded bundle
\sigma^{k}:F^{k}\rightarrow F^{0}
;
\sigma^{k}
is a VB morphism and
\pi^{k}
is a morphism of graded bundles. In the special case
k=1
, we recover the notion of a
double vector bundle
(DVB, in short), e.g.
[
Mac05
]
.

Given a graded bundle
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
of order
k
and an integer
0\leq j\leq k
we may consider a natural projection, denoted by
\sigma^{k}_{j}:E^{k}\rightarrow E^{j}
, where
E^{j}
is a graded bundle of order
j
over
M
obtained from
E^{k}
by
removing from the atlas for
E^{k}
all coordinates of weights greater than
j
. The graded bundle
E^{j}
obtained this way is denoted by
E^{k}[\Delta\leq j]

[
BGR16
]
and called
the reduction of
E^{k}
to order
j
. Taking
j=k-1,k-2,\ldots,0
we arrive at the
tower of affine bundle projections

E^{k}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{k}_{k-1}}E^{k-1}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{k-1}_{k-2}}E^{k-2}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{k-2}_{k-3}}\ldots\xrightarrow{\sigma^{2}_{1}}E^{1}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{1}_{0}}M=E^{0}.

We have
\sigma^{k}_{j}=\sigma^{j+1}_{j}\circ\ldots\circ\sigma^{k}_{k-1}
, and we write shortly
\sigma^{j}
for
\sigma^{j}_{0}
.

A complementary construction is obtained by setting to zero all fiber coordinates in the bundle
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
of weight less than a given number
1<j\leq k
. The resulting submanifold, denoted by
E^{k}[\Delta\geq j]
, is a graded subbundle of
E^{k}
with the same base
M
. In case
j=k
,
E^{k}[\Delta\geq k]
is called the
core
of
E^{k}
and denoted by
\widehat{E^{k}}
. The core can be endowed with a natural VB structure. This way we obtain a functor
\widehat{\cdot}:\mathcal{GB}[k]\rightarrow\mathcal{VB}
, where
\mathcal{GB}[k]
is the category of graded bundles of order
k
, and
\mathcal{VB}=\mathcal{GB}[1]
is the category of vector bundles. In the case of multi-graded structures
(F;\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{n})
, we write
F\in\mathcal{GB}[k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}]
, indicating that
(F,\Delta_{i})\in\mathcal{GB}[k_{i}]
and
[\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}]=0
for
i\neq j
. The core of the graded bundle
(F,\Delta_{1}+\ldots+\Delta_{n})
is denoted in the same way as
\widehat{F}
. (It will be usually clear which weight field of
F
we are referring to.)

There is an obvious graded bundle structure on the product
E\times F
of the graded bundles
E
and
F
, defined by
h^{E\times F}_{t}=h^{E}_{t}\times h^{F}_{t}
where
t\in\mathbb{R}
. If
E,F
have the same base
M
, then
E\times_{M}F
is a graded subbundle of
E\times F
.

Given a positive integer
k
and a vector bundle
E\rightarrow M
we write
E_{[k]}
for the graded bundle
(E,k\cdot\Delta)
, where
\Delta
is the Euler vector field of
E
. Then, for example,
E_{[1]}\times_{M}F_{[2]}
refers to a graded bundle of order two. It is the graded bundle associated with the graded vector bundle
E_{[1]}\oplus F_{[2]}
, where
E
,
F
are VBs over
M
.

2.2
Double vector bundles and VB-algebroids

As we already mentioned, a structure of a DVB on a manifold
D
is a pair of VBs
\sigma_{E}:D\to E
and
\sigma_{A}:D\to A
such that for any
x\in D
and
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
holds

t\cdot_{E}(s\cdot_{A}x)=s\cdot_{A}(t\cdot_{E}x)

where
\cdot_{E}
(respectively,
\cdot_{A}
) denotes the multiplication by scalars in the vector bundle
\sigma_{E}
(resp.,
\sigma_{A}
).
The bases
E
and
A
carry induced VB structures over a common base
M
giving rise to a diagram

\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{E}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{A}_{M}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{E}_{M}}
\textstyle{M.}

(2.2)

There is also a third vector bundle over
M
, known as
the core
of the DVB
(D,\sigma_{E},\sigma_{A})
, defined as the intersection of the kernels of the VB morphisms
\sigma_{E}
and
\sigma_{A}
,
C=\ker\sigma_{E}\cap\ker\sigma_{A}
. From the perspective of graded manifolds, DVBs are
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
-graded manifolds admitting coordinates only in weights
(0,0)
,
(1,0)
,
(0,1)
and
(1,1)
. From this perspective, the core
C
is the core of the graded bundle
(D,\Delta_{E}+\Delta_{A})
, where
\Delta_{E}
(resp.,
\Delta_{A}
) is the Euler vector field of the vector bundle
\sigma_{E}
(resp.,
\sigma_{A}
), and it will be denoted simply as
C=\widehat{D}
.

There is a well-defined action
D\times_{M}C\rightarrow D
, denoted by
(d,c)\mapsto d\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c
, which arises from the affine bundle structure of
D
over its order-one reduction,
E\times_{M}A
.
A section
c\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
gives a so-called
core section

c^{\dagger}
of the VB
\sigma_{E}:D\to E
, given by
\sigma(e_{m})=e_{m}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c(m)
where
m\in M
,
e_{m}\in(\sigma^{E}_{M})^{-1}(m)
.
A section
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}_{E}(D)
is called
linear
if it is a VB morphism from
(E,\sigma^{E}_{M})
to
(D,\sigma_{A})
. The subspace of linear sections (resp. core sections) is denoted by
\operatorname{\Gamma}^{\ell}_{E}(D)
(resp.,
\operatorname{\Gamma}^{c}_{E}(D)
).

A
decomposition
of a DVB
D
as in (
2.2
) is a DVB morphism from
D
to its
split form

\bar{D}:=E\times_{M}A\times_{M}C

which is the identity on each component: the
s
ide bundles
E
,
A
and the core
C
. Decompositions are in bijective correspondence with
inclusions
(also referred to as decompositions)
\sum:A\times_{M}E\to D
, which are DVB morphisms inducing the identity on the side bundles
A
and
E
. Decompositions are also in bijective correspondence with
horizontal lifts

\theta_{A}:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\to\operatorname{\Gamma}^{\ell}_{E}(D)
, which are defined as splittings of the short exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{Hom}(E,C)\to\operatorname{\Gamma}^{\ell}_{E}(D)\to\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\to 0

(2.3)

of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-modules where
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}^{\ell}_{E}(D)
projects to its base map, which turns out to be a section of
\sigma^{A}_{M}
.

The foundation on DVBs were laid by J. Pradines
[
Pra75
]
. Double structures such as DVBs, as well as double Lie groupoids and algebroids where extensively studied by K. C. H. Mackenzie and his collaborators (see
[
Mac05
]
and references therein). In this paper, we shall deal with VB-algebroids – a pair of an algebroid and a VB structures, in compatibility, defined on a common manifold.

The compatibility condition can be stated in various equivalent ways, presenting such a structure as a Lie algebroid object in the category of vector bundles (the origins of the notion of VB-algebroids) or as a vector bundle object in the category of Lie algebroids (LA-vector bundles). See
[
GSM10
]
for definitions and the equivalence of both concepts.

Following the ideas from
[
GR09
]
, one can formulate the compatibility condition as follows:
a VB-algebroid structure on a manifold
D
is a pair of VBs
\sigma_{A}:D\rightarrow A
,
\sigma_{E}:D\rightarrow E
, and a Lie algebroid structure on the vector bundle
\sigma_{E}
, such that for each
t\in\mathbb{R}
the map
x\mapsto t\cdot_{A}x
,
x\in D
, is an algebroid morphism, see
[
BGV18
, Definition 2.10]
.

It follows that
(D,\sigma_{E},\sigma_{A})
is a DVB;
\sigma^{A}_{M}:A\to M
carries an induced algebroid structure. Moreover, the anchor map
\sharp^{D}:D\to\mathrm{T}E
is a DVB morphism, and
\operatorname{\Gamma}^{c}_{E}(D)\oplus\operatorname{\Gamma}^{\ell}_{E}(D)
is a graded Lie algebra, concentrated in degrees
-1
(the space of core sections) and
0
(the space of linear sections), with respect to the Lie bracket on
\operatorname{\Gamma}(\sigma_{E})
.

2.3
Linearisation of graded bundles and the functor
\lambda

We define a functor
\lambda:\mathcal{GB}[k,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{GB}[k-1,1]
. It is slightly more general then the functor of linearisation
{\operatorname{lin}}:\mathcal{GB}[k]\rightarrow\mathcal{GB}[k-1,1]
introduced in
[
BGG16
]
. Actually,
{\operatorname{lin}}=\lambda\circ\mathrm{T}
is the composition of the tangent functor
\mathrm{T}:\mathcal{GB}[k]\rightarrow\mathcal{GB}[k,1]
with the functor
\lambda
. The construction of the functor
\lambda
is given in two steps.
In the first step, we set to zero all coordinates for
F^{k}\in\mathcal{GB}[k,1]
of weight
(0,1)
. After shifting in weight by
(-1,0)
, the target
\lambda(F^{k})
is obtained from the latter by removing coordinates of weight
(k,0)
.

Definition 2.1

(Functor
\lambda
)
.

Let
(F^{k};\Delta^{k}_{1},\Delta^{1}_{2})
be a weighted vector bundle
as in (
2.1
), where
(F^{k},\Delta^{k}_{1})\in\mathcal{GB}[k]
and
(F^{k},\Delta^{1}_{2})
is a vector bundle.
Let
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}
denote the kernel of the VB morphism
\sigma^{k}:F^{k}\to F^{0}
.
Although
\Delta:=\Delta_{1}^{k}-\Delta_{2}^{1}
is not a combination
with non-negative coefficients, it is a weight vector field on the submanifold
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}\subseteq F^{k}
.
We define the graded bundle
\lambda(F^{k})
as
the reduction of the graded bundle
(\lambda^{\mathrm{v}},\Delta|_{\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}})
from order
k
to
k-1
,

{\lambda(F^{k}):=\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}[\Delta|_{\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}}\leq k-1],\text{ where }\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}=\ker\sigma^{k}.}

(2.4)

In other words, we set to zero the coordinates of weight
(0,1)
and then we remove the coordinates of weight
(k,0)
.
Consider the following diagrams:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 15.28793pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.28793pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lambda(F^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.28793pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.28793pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lambda(F^{1})\simeq\widehat{F^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.23537pt\raise-26.04831pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-11.13483pt\raise-32.8858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\underline{F}^{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.28123pt\raise-32.8858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 51.28123pt\raise-32.8858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

In the diagram on the left, the projection
\ker\sigma^{k}\to\lambda(F^{k})
is a fiber-wise linear isomorphism, so
\ker\sigma^{k}
is the pullback of the vector bundle
\lambda(F^{k})
with respect to the projection
\underline{\sigma}^{k}_{k-1}
.

In the diagram on the right,
\lambda(F^{k})\in\mathcal{GB}[k-1,1]
is recognized as a weighted vector bundle whose weight vector fields are inherited from
\Delta
and
\Delta_{2}^{1}
. The base of the graded bundle
\lambda(F^{k})
is identified as the core of the DVB
F^{1}\in\mathcal{GB}[1,1]
. If
(x^{a},y^{i}_{(\alpha,\beta)})
are graded coordinates on the weighted VB
F^{k}
, then the adapted coordinates on
\lambda(F^{k})
are obtained by omitting those
y^{i}_{(\alpha,\beta)}
with
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(k,0),(0,1)\}
, and the coordinates of weight
(w,1)
are assigned a new weight
(w-1,1)
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
be a graded bundle of order
k
. There are canonical isomorphism of weighted vector bundles:

(i)

If
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
is a graded bundle of order
k
, then
\lambda(\mathrm{T}E^{k})\simeq{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
.

(ii)

If
\sigma:E\rightarrow M
is a vector bundle, then
\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}E)\simeq\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E
.

Proof.

Only (
ii
) needs a proof, as (
i
) follows directly from the construction of
\lambda
and the linearisation functor.

For the proof of (
ii
), observe that the inclusion
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}(\mathrm{T}^{k}E)\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}E
is realized by the mapping

\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\times_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M}\mathrm{T}^{k}M\hookrightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}E,\hskip 9.24994pt([a]_{k-1},[\gamma]_{k})\mapsto[t\mapsto ta(t)]_{k},

(2.5)

where curves
a:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow E
and
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow M
are such that
\sigma\circ a=\gamma
. Indeed, the image of the mapping (
2.5
) is the subbundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{M}E\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}E
. In the standard local coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i})
on
E
, it is given by the vanishing coordinates of weight
(0,1)
, i.e.,
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}(\mathrm{T}^{k}E)=\mathrm{T}^{k}_{M}E=\{(x^{a,({\alpha})},y^{i,({\beta})}):y^{i,(0)}=0\}
.

Finally, we realize that the canonical projection
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}(\mathrm{T}^{k}E)\rightarrow\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}E)
defined locally by removing coordinates of weight
(k,0)
, i.e., the coordinates
x^{a,(k)}
, coincides with the projection

\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\times_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M}\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E,\hskip 9.24994pt([a]_{k-1},[\gamma]_{k})\mapsto[a]_{k-1}.

From (
2.5
) we easily find that the obtained isomorphism
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E\rightarrow\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}E)
, denoted by
I^{k}_{E}
, has the formula

{{(I^{k}_{E})}^{*}(x^{a,({\alpha})})=x^{a,({\alpha})},\hskip 9.24994pt{(I^{k}_{E})}^{*}(y^{i,({\beta})})={\beta}\dot{y}^{i,({\beta}-1)}}

(2.6)

where
(x^{a,({\alpha})},y^{i,({\beta})})
,
0\leq{\alpha}\leq k-1
,
1\leq{\beta}\leq k
, are the coordinates for
\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}E)
inherited from
\mathrm{T}^{k}E
.
∎

2.4
Natural inclusions and isomorphisms I

For later use, we shall fix the natural inclusions:

j^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}M,[\gamma]_{1}\mapsto[t\mapsto\gamma(t^{k}/k!)]_{k},

(2.7)

and

i^{k,l}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k+l}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}^{l}M,[\gamma]_{k+l}\longmapsto[t\mapsto[s\mapsto\gamma(t+s)]_{l}]_{k},

(2.8)

so
i^{k,l}_{M}([\gamma]_{k+l})=[\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{l}\gamma]_{k}={\left(\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{l}\gamma\right)(0)}
where
\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{l}\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{l}M
is the
l
-th tangent lift of the curve
\gamma
.
In coordinates,

j^{k}_{M}(x^{a},\dot{x}^{a})=(x^{a},0,\ldots 0,\dot{x}^{a}),

(2.9)

(i^{k,l}_{M})^{\ast}(x^{a,{({\alpha},{\beta})}})=x^{a,({\alpha}+{\beta})}.

(2.10)

where
x^{a,({\alpha},{\beta})}=\left(x^{a,({\alpha})}\right)^{({\beta})}
. In addition to
j^{k}_{M}
, given a vector bundle
\sigma:E\rightarrow M
, there is a canonical VB isomorphism of the core bundle of
(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}}\Delta_{E}+\Delta_{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E})
and the vector bundle
(E,\Delta_{E})
which is defined by

\jmath^{k}_{E}:E\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}E,\hskip 9.24994ptv\mapsto[t\mapsto\frac{t^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}v]_{k-1}

(2.11)

The compatibility with the map
j^{k}_{M}
is expressed by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{k}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\jmath^{k-1}_{\mathrm{T}M}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{k-1,1}_{M}}
\textstyle{T^{k-1}\mathrm{T}M}

(2.12)

A graded bundle
(E^{k},\Delta)
embeds naturally into its linearisation via the digitalisation map

\mathrm{diag}^{k}:E^{k}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{lin}}{E^{k}},\hskip 9.24994pt(\mathrm{diag}^{k})^{*}(\dot{y}^{i}_{w})=wy^{i}_{w},\,1\leq w\leq k

(2.13)

in the adapted coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i^{\prime}}_{w^{\prime}};\dot{y}^{i}_{w})
, where
1\leq w\leq k
,
1\leq w^{\prime}\leq k-1
, on
{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})=\lambda(\mathrm{T}E^{k})
induced from
\mathrm{T}E^{k}
, as mentioned earlier. Moreover,
\mathrm{diag}^{k}
covers the identity over
E^{k-1}
.
This map is induced from the weight vector field considered as a map
\Delta:E^{k}\rightarrow\mathbf{V}E^{k}
, where
\mathbf{V}E^{k}=\ker\mathrm{T}\sigma^{k}
denotes the
vertical
subbundle of
\mathrm{T}E^{k}
. In other words, the weight vector field
\Delta
is projectable with respect to the canonical projection
\mathbf{V}E^{k}\rightarrow{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
.
Moreover, in the special case
E^{k}=\mathrm{T}^{k}M
, the map
\mathrm{diag}^{k}
coincides with
i^{1,k-1}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
composed with the inverse of the isomorphism
I:{\operatorname{lin}}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M)=\lambda(\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M)\xrightarrow{\lambda(\kappa^{k}_{M})}\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}M)\xrightarrow{I^{k}_{\mathrm{T}M}}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}M\simeq\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
, where
I^{k}_{\mathrm{T}M}
is the isomorphism established in Lemma
2.2
(
ii
). The isomorphism
I:{\operatorname{lin}}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M)\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
coincides with the isomorphism found in (
[
BGG15b
, Example 2.2.3]
,
[
BGR16
]
) and is given by

I^{\ast}(\mathrm{d}x^{a,({\alpha})})=\frac{1}{{\alpha}+1}\mathrm{d}x^{a,({\alpha}+1)}

for
{\alpha}=0,1,\ldots,k-1
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\sigma_{i}:E^{k}_{i}\rightarrow M_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, be graded bundles of order
k
and let
\phi:E^{k}_{1}\rightarrow E^{k}_{2}
be a
\mathcal{GB}[k]
-morphisms. Then the linearisation of
\phi
intertwines the canonical inclusions
\mathrm{diag}_{i}:E_{i}^{k}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k}_{i})
:

\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(\phi)}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k}_{2})}
\textstyle{E^{k}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{diag}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{E^{k}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{diag}_{2}}

Proof.

The map
{\operatorname{lin}}(\phi)
is the unique map which makes the following diagram commutative:

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}E^{k}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\phi}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}E^{k}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(\phi)}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k}_{2})}

where
\mathbf{V}E^{k}_{i}=\ker\mathrm{T}\sigma_{i}\subseteq\mathrm{T}E^{k}_{i}
. The weight vector fields
\Delta_{1}
,
\Delta_{2}
are
\phi
-related as
\phi:E^{k}_{1}\rightarrow E^{k}_{2}
is a
\mathcal{GB}[k]
-morphism (
[
GR11
, Theorem 2.3]
). Hence
\mathrm{T}\phi\circ\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}\circ\phi
, and the thesis follows directly from the definition of the diagonalisation map.
∎

2.5
Vector bundle comorphisms

We shall recall the definition of a comorphism between vector bundles from
[
JR18
]
where one can also find more information and references on the origins and generalizations of this concept.

Definition 2.4
.

A
vector bundle comorphism
(VB comorphism, for short), from a vector bundle
\sigma_{1}:E_{1}\rightarrow M_{1}
to a vector bundle
\sigma_{2}:E_{2}\rightarrow M_{2}
, is a relation
r\subset E_{1}\times E_{2}
, for which there exist a
base map

\underline{r}:M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}
and a VB morphism
r^{!}:{\underline{r}}^{*}E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
covering the identity on
M_{2}
such that

r=\{(v,r^{!}(v,y)):v\in E_{1},y\in M_{2},\sigma_{1}(v)=\underline{r}(y)\}

where
{\underline{r}}^{*}E_{1}\subset E_{1}\times M_{2}
is the pullback of the vector bundle
\sigma_{1}
with respect to the map
\underline{r}
.
We say that the base map
\underline{r}:M_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}
(which is uniquely defined)
covers

r
, and we depict this in the following diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 10.1291pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.83466pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.41875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.20047pt\raise 4.35625pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.35625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.17355pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.5pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-4.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 35.42355pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.25821pt\raise-15.41875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.25821pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-10.1291pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M_{1}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 34.1291pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.6291pt\raise-24.99861pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\underline{r}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.12912pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Thus,
r
is the union of graphs of linear maps
r_{y}:(E_{1})_{\underline{r}(y)}\rightarrow(E_{2})_{y}
between
the corresponding fibers, where
y
varies in
M_{2}
.
There is a one-to-one correspondence between VB comorphisms
\sigma_{1}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\rhd}\sigma_{2}
and VB morphisms
\sigma_{2}^{\ast}\rightarrow\sigma_{1}^{\ast}
between the dual bundles.
A VB comorphism
r:\sigma_{1}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\rhd}\sigma_{2}
gives rise to a mapping between the spaces of sections,

{\overrightarrow{r}}:\operatorname{\Gamma}(\sigma_{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(\sigma_{2}),\hskip 9.24994pt{\overrightarrow{r}}(s)(y)=r_{y}(s(\underline{r}(y))).

The map
{\overrightarrow{r}}
satisfies

{\overrightarrow{r}}(s+s^{\prime})={\overrightarrow{r}}(s)+{\overrightarrow{r}}(s^{\prime}),\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt{\overrightarrow{r}}(f\cdot s)=\underline{r}^{\ast}(f)\cdot{\overrightarrow{s}}

(2.14)

and any such map gives rise to a VB comorphism
r:\sigma_{1}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\rhd}\sigma_{2}
.

VB comorphisms form a category denoted by
\mathcal{VBC}
. A morphism from
r\in\mathcal{VBC}
to
r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{VBC}
, where

r:\sigma_{1}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\rhd}\sigma_{2}
and
r^{\prime}:\sigma_{1}^{\prime}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\rhd}\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
are VB comorphism and
\sigma_{i}:E_{i}\rightarrow M_{i}
,
\sigma_{i}^{\prime}:E_{i}^{\prime}\rightarrow M_{i}^{\prime}
are vector bundles, is given by a pair
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
of VB morphisms
\phi_{i}:E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i}^{\prime}
such that
(\phi_{1}\times\phi_{2})(r)\subset r^{\prime}
(
[
JR13
, Definition 2.3 and Proposition 2.6]
). It is denoted by
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2}):r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}
.

A VB comorphism
r:\sigma_{1}{-\!\!\!-\!\!\rhd}\sigma_{2}
is
weighted
of order
k
if the total spaces
E_{1},E_{2}
are given a structure of a graded bundle of order
k
with respect to which
r
is a graded subbundle of the product
E_{1}\times E_{2}
.

We shall need the following result in Section
3
. Roughly speaking, it states that
\lambda
is also a functor on the category of weighted vector bundle comorphisms.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
F_{1}^{k},F_{2}^{k}\in\mathcal{GB}[k,1]
be weighted, order
k
, vector bundles and let
\pi_{i}:F_{i}^{k}\rightarrow\underline{F}_{i}^{k}
denotes the corresponding VB projections. Let
r^{k}:\pi_{1}{\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}\pi_{2}
be a weighted, order
k
, VB comorphism covering
\underline{r}^{k}:\underline{F}_{2}^{k}\rightarrow\underline{F}_{1}^{k}
. Then

\lambda(r^{k}):\lambda(F_{1}^{k}){\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}\lambda(F_{2}^{k})
is a VB comorphism covering
\underline{r}^{k-1}:\underline{F}_{2}^{k-1}\rightarrow\underline{F}_{1}^{k-1}
:

\textstyle{\lambda(F_{1}^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(r^{k})}
\textstyle{\lambda(F_{2}^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{F}_{1}^{k-1}}
\textstyle{\underline{F}_{2}^{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{r}^{k-1}}

Moreover, if
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2}):r\Rightarrow r^{\prime}
is a morphism between weighted VB comorphisms
r:F_{1}{\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}F_{2}
and
r^{\prime}:{F_{1}^{\prime}}{\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}{F_{2}^{\prime}}
then
(\lambda(\phi_{1}),\lambda(\phi_{2})):\lambda(r)\Rightarrow\lambda(r^{\prime})
is the same.

Proof.

Note that
r^{k}
is a weighted vector subbundle of
F_{1}^{k}\times F_{2}^{k}
, hence
\lambda(r^{k})
is a weighted vector subbundle of
\lambda(F_{1}^{k}\times F_{2}^{k})=\lambda(F_{1}^{k})\times\lambda(F^{2}_{k})
. Let us trace the subsequent steps of the construction of the weighted vector bundle
\lambda(r^{k})
, as in Definition
2.1
. We have
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}(r^{k})=r^{k}\cap(\ker\sigma^{k}_{1}\times\ker\sigma^{k}_{2})
where
\sigma_{i}^{k}:F_{i}^{k}\rightarrow F^{0}_{i}
are as in (
2.1
) for
i=1,2
. Hence,
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}(r^{k})
is a VB comorphism
\ker\sigma^{k}_{1}{\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}\ker\sigma^{k}_{2}
covering
\underline{r}^{k}:\underline{F}_{2}^{k}\rightarrow\underline{F}_{1}^{k}
. The goal
\lambda(r^{k})
is obtained from
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}(r^{k})
by the reduction to order
k-1
of the base map
\underline{r}^{k}
. Since the projections
\lambda^{\mathrm{v}}(F^{k}_{i})\rightarrow\lambda(F^{k}_{i})
are fiber-wise linear isomorphisms,
\lambda(r^{k})
remains a VB comorphism.

For the last part of Lemma, we have already noticed that the functor
\lambda
preserves the products and inclusions. By
[
JR18
, Proposition 2.6]

(\phi_{1}\times\phi_{2})(r)\subseteq r^{\prime}
, hence
(\lambda(\phi_{1})\times\lambda(\phi_{2}))(\lambda(r))\subseteq r^{\prime}
, so
(\lambda(\phi_{1}),\lambda(\phi_{2}))
is a morphism in the category
\mathcal{VBC}
.
∎

The core
\widehat{E^{k}}
acts naturally on the graded bundle
E^{k}
.
This action

E^{k}\times_{M}\widehat{E^{k}}\to E^{k}
is denoted by
(a^{k},v)\mapsto a^{k}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}v\in E^{k}
and gives rise to a VB comorphism,

\textstyle{v\in\widehat{E^{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ni{v^{\uparrow}}(a^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}
\textstyle{E^{k}\ni a^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{k}}

(2.15)

where
{v^{\uparrow}}(a^{k})\in\mathrm{T}_{a^{k}}E^{k}
is the vector represented by the curve
t\mapsto a^{k}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}(tv)
.
In coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i}_{w},z^{\mu}_{k})
on
E^{k}
, where
y^{i}_{w}
’s have weights
1\leq w\leq k-1
, and
\mathrm{w}(z^{\mu}_{k})=k
, the associated map on sections is given by
v\mapsto{v^{\uparrow}}=v^{\mu}(x)\partial_{z^{\mu}_{k}}
, where
v=\sum_{\mu}v^{\mu}(x)c_{\mu}
and
(c_{\mu})
is a local frame of
\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}})
. Since
E^{k}\rightarrow E^{k-1}
is an affine bundle modelled on the pullback of the core
\widehat{E^{k}}\rightarrow M
, there is a map

E^{k}\times_{E^{k-1}}E^{k}\rightarrow\widehat{E^{k}},\hskip 9.24994pt(a^{\prime},a)\mapsto a^{\prime}\bm{\boldsymbol{-}}a,

(2.16)

where
a^{\prime}-a
is the unique vector
v\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}})
such that
a\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}v=a^{\prime}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

The mapping associated with the VB comorphism (
2.15
),

\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{-k}(E^{k}),v\mapsto{v^{\uparrow}},

(2.17)

is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-module isomorphism.
Moreover, if
\sigma_{i}:E^{k}_{i}\rightarrow M_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, are graded bundles of order
k
and
\phi:E^{k}_{1}\rightarrow E^{k}_{2}
is a
\mathcal{GB}[k]
-morphism then weight
-k
vector fields
X_{i}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-k}(E^{k}_{1})
are
\phi
-related if and only if
the corresponding sections
v_{i}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}_{i}})
are
\widehat{\phi}
-related. If
M_{1}=M_{2}
and
\phi
covers the identity, then the last condition means that
v_{2}=\widehat{\phi}\circ v_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
v_{i}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}_{i}})
,
X_{i}={v_{i}^{\uparrow}}
for
i=1,2
. The vector fields
X_{i}
are represented by the families of curves
t\mapsto a_{i}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}tv_{i}(\underline{a_{i}})
where
a_{i}\in E_{i}^{k}
and
\underline{a_{i}}=\sigma^{k}_{i}(a_{i})\in M_{i}
.
The sections
v_{1},v_{2}
are
\widehat{\phi}
-related if and only if
v_{2}(m_{2})=\widehat{\phi}(v_{1}(m_{1}))
for any pair
(m_{1},m_{2})
such that
\underline{\phi}(m_{1})=m_{2}
. Note that
\phi(a_{1}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}tv_{1}(\underline{a_{1}}))=\phi(a_{1})\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}t\widehat{\phi}(v_{1}(\underline{a_{1}}))
, hence if
v_{1},v_{2}
are
\widehat{\phi}
-related then
(\mathrm{T}\phi)X_{1}(a_{1})=X_{2}(\phi(a_{2}))
. Thus,
X_{1},X_{2}
are
\phi
-related. The proof in the converse direction is very similar and is left to the reader.
∎

2.6
Higher algebroids

It is well-known that a Lie algebroid
(\sigma:A\to M,[\cdot,\cdot],\sharp)
can be represented as a linear Poisson tensor on
A^{\ast}
, the total space of the dual vector bundle. This, in turn, gives rise to a VB morphism
\varepsilon:\mathrm{T}^{\ast}A\to\mathrm{T}A^{\ast}
which is a Poisson map and retains all the information about the algebroid structure on
A
. The dual of
\varepsilon
is a VB comorphism
\kappa:\mathrm{T}\sigma{\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}\tau_{A}
which was a starting point in the concept of HAs originated in
[
JR13
]
.

A general algebroid structure on a vector bundle
\sigma:E\rightarrow M
can be encoded as a VB comorphism
\kappa:\mathrm{T}\sigma{-\!\!\!-\!\!\rhd}\tau_{E}
of a special kind, see
[
JR18
]
, Proposition 2.15. In this correspondence

\kappa
should be also a vector subbundle of
\tau_{E}\times\mathrm{T}\sigma
, and the induced VB morphism between the core bundles should be the identity,

\widehat{\kappa}=\operatorname{id}_{\widehat{\mathrm{T}E}}

(2.18)

Let us recall that the core of the DVB

\mathrm{T}E
is the subbundle
\mathbf{V}_{M}E
of the vertical bundle
\mathbf{V}E
of
E
, and it is naturally identified with the vector bundle
E
itself.
Moreover, algebroid morphisms
\phi:(E_{1},\kappa_{1})\rightarrow(E_{2},\kappa_{2})
are in a one-to-one correspondence with
\mathcal{VBC}
-morphisms
(\mathrm{T}\phi,\mathrm{T}\phi):\kappa_{1}\Rightarrow\kappa_{2}
.
The above concept of an algebroid has a direct analogue in higher-order, which we shall recall now.

Definition 2.7
.

[
JR18
]
A general (
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order) higher algebroid (HA, in short) is a
graded bundle
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
of order
k
together with a weighted VB comorphism
\kappa^{k}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\times\mathrm{T}E^{k}
from
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}
to
\tau_{E^{k}}
(covering a mapping
\sharp^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
) such that the relation
\kappa^{1}:\mathrm{T}\sigma^{1}{\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}\mathrm{T}\tau_{E^{1}}
, being the reduction to order one of
\kappa^{k}
, equips
\sigma^{1}:E^{1}\rightarrow M
with an algebroid structure:

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{E^{k}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces E^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{k}}

(2.19)

In addition:

(i)

If
\kappa^{1}
is a symmetric relation, then the HA
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
is called
skew
.
9
9

9

This is equivalent to saying that the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
on
\operatorname{\Gamma}(E^{1})
is skew-symmetric, see
[
JR18
]
.

(ii)

If
(\sigma^{1},\kappa^{1})
is skew and, in addition, the diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sharp^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\sharp^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}_{M}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M}

(2.20)

is commutative, i.e.,
(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sharp^{1},\mathrm{T}\sharp^{k}):\kappa^{k}\Rightarrow\kappa^{k}_{M}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{VBC}
, then we call
(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k})
an
almost Lie higher algebroid
;

(iii)

Both vector bundles,
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}
and
\tau_{E^{k}}
in the diagram (
2.19
), carry a canonical algebroid structure.
10
10

10

The
k
-tangent lift of
(\sigma^{1},\kappa^{1})
gives an algebroid structure on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}
.
If
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
is a skew HA and
\kappa^{k}
is a subalgebroid of the product of these algebroids then
(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k})
is called a
Lie
HA.
11
11

11

This condition can be restated as the dual VB morphism
\varepsilon^{k}:\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}(E^{1})^{\ast}
is a Poisson map, e.g.
[
Gra12
]
. Moreover, a Lie HA has to be AL, i.e., the condition (
iii
) implies (
ii
), see
[
JR18
]
.

A
morphism
between higher algebroids
(\sigma^{k}_{E}:E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k,E})
and
(\sigma^{k}_{F}:F^{k}\rightarrow N,\kappa^{k,F})
is a morphism of graded bundles
\phi^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow F^{k}
such that
(\mathrm{T}^{k}\phi^{1},\mathrm{T}\phi^{k}):\kappa^{k,E}\Rightarrow\kappa^{k,F}
is a
\mathcal{VBC}
-morphism. Higher algebroids with
\mathcal{VBC}
-morphisms form a category. The reduction of a HA
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
to a lower order
j
,
1\leq j<k
gives a HA denoted by
(E^{j},\kappa^{j})
which is skew (resp. AL, Lie) if
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
was so.

Example 2.8

(
[
JR18
]
HAs of order
2
, in coordinates)
.

Let
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu})
be local graded coordinates on a graded bundle
\sigma^{2}:E^{2}\rightarrow M
of order
2
.
Taking into account only the graded bundle structure of
\kappa^{2}
, we obtain the following system of equations for
\kappa^{2}\subset\mathrm{T}^{2}E^{1}\times_{M}\mathrm{T}E^{2}\in\mathcal{GB}[1,2]
. (We have underlined the coordinates on
\mathrm{T}E^{2}
in order to distinguish them from the coordinates on
\mathrm{T}^{2}E^{1}
.)

\kappa^{2}:\begin{cases}\dot{x}^{a}=&Q^{a}_{i}\,\underline{y}^{i}\\
\ddot{x}^{a}=&\frac{1}{2}\,Q^{a}_{ij}\,\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}+Q^{a}_{\mu}\,\underline{z}^{\mu},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ where }Q^{a}_{ij}=Q^{a}_{ji},\\
\dot{\underline{x}}^{a}=&\tilde{Q}^{a}_{i}\,y^{i}\\
\dot{\underline{y}}^{i}=&Q^{i}_{j}\dot{y}^{j}+Q^{i}_{jk}\,\underline{y}^{j}y^{k},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ where }Q^{i}_{j}=\delta^{i}_{j},\\
\dot{\underline{z}}^{\mu}=&Q^{\mu}_{i}\,\ddot{y}^{i}+Q^{\mu}_{ij}\,\underline{y}^{i}\dot{y}^{j}+Q^{\mu}_{\nu i}\,\underline{z}^{\nu}y^{i}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}y^{k},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ where }Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}=Q^{\mu}_{ji,k},\end{cases}

(2.21)

for some
structure functions

Q^{\cdots}_{\cdots}
. The condition
Q^{i}_{j}=\delta^{i}_{j}
corresponds to (
2.18
) and it ensures that the order-one reduction of
\kappa^{2}
gives a (general) algebroid structure on
A=E^{1}
. If
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
is a skew HA then
\tilde{Q}^{a}_{i}=Q^{a}_{i}
and
Q^{i}_{jk}=-Q^{i}_{kj}
since
\kappa^{1}
is a symmetric relation. The structure functions satisfy certain equations reflecting the axioms of a higher-order algebroid. These equations are derived in Appendix, Subsection
4.3
.

Example 2.9
.

The natural diffeomorphism
\kappa^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}M
defines a Lie, order
k
HA on
\tau^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
. Indeed,
(\mathrm{T}^{k}M,\kappa^{k}_{M})
satisfies the Lie condition (Definition
2.7
(
iii
)) because
\varepsilon^{k}_{M}:=(\kappa^{k}_{M})^{\ast}:\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}^{\ast}M
is a Poisson map. It also comes from a more general result, see
[
JR18
, Proposition 4.13]
.

2.7
Reformulation of the definition of a HA in terms of algebroid lifts

We shall review the construction of higher lifts
s^{({\alpha})}
of sections of a vector bundle. This notion is used in various parts of this work, such as in the definition of algebroid lifts
{s}^{\langle{{\alpha}-k}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}(E^{k})
(see (
2.29
)), which facilitate the convenient description of the axioms of HAs (see Theorem
2.11
).

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(\sigma)
be a section of a vector bundle
\sigma:E\to M
. We can interpret
s
as a linear function
\iota(s)
on
E^{\ast}
, the linear dual of
E
. Let
0\leq{\alpha}\leq k
. Then the
({\alpha})
-lift of
\iota(s)
is a function on
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}
, commuting with
h^{\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}}_{t}=\mathrm{T}^{k}(h^{E^{\ast}}_{t})
, the homogeneity structure on
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}
. Therefore,
\iota(s)^{({\alpha})}
can be interpreted as a section of the linear dual of the vector bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{\ast}:\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
, which is identified with the vector bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma:\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
via the non-degenerate pairing

\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma}:\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}\times_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\mathrm{T}^{k}E\simeq\mathrm{T}^{k}(E^{\ast}\times_{M}E)\xrightarrow{\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\sigma}^{(k)}}\mathbb{R},

(2.22)

obtained as
(k)
-lift of the pairing
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\sigma}:E^{\ast}\times_{M}E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. The section of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
obtained this way is denoted by
s^{(\alpha)}
and called
the
({\alpha})
-lift of the section
s
. In standard coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i})
on
E
, and
(x^{a},\xi_{i})
on
E^{\ast}
, where
\xi_{i}=\iota(e_{i})
, the
(k)
-lift of the function
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{\sigma}=y^{i}\xi_{i}
is obtained using the general Leibniz rule, and has the form

\langle{(x^{a,({\alpha})},\xi_{i}^{({\beta})}),(x^{a,({\alpha})},y^{i,({\beta})})}\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma}=\sum_{{\alpha}=0}^{k}\binom{k}{{\alpha}}\xi_{i}^{({\alpha})}y^{i,(k-{\alpha})}.

It follows that the family
(e_{i}^{({\alpha})})
, where
0\leq\alpha\leq k
and
\iota(e_{i}^{({\alpha})})=\xi_{i}^{({\alpha})}
, forms a local frame of sections of the vector bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
. Moreover,
\langle{y^{j,(\beta)},e_{i}^{(k-\alpha)}}\rangle_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma}=\delta^{i}_{j}\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\binom{k}{\alpha}
, hence

y^{i,({\alpha})}\circ e_{i}^{(k-{\alpha})}=\binom{k}{{\alpha}}^{-1},

(2.23)

as the composition of functions
e_{i}^{(k-{\alpha})}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}E
and
y^{i,({\alpha})}:T^{k}E\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. From this it is straightforward to verify that this construction of

s^{({\alpha})}
is equivalent to the one presented in
[
JR18
]
. We have

(f\cdot s)^{(\beta)}=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\beta}\binom{\beta}{\alpha}f^{(\alpha)}s^{(\beta-\alpha)}

(2.24)

for
\beta=0,1,\ldots,k
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
and
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E)
. This is simply the Leibniz rule for the iterated derivative.

Definition 2.10

(Vertical lifts)
.

Let
0\leq\alpha\leq k
. We define a VB comorphism
\mathbf{V}^{k}_{\alpha}
,

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{\alpha}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{V}^{k}_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{\alpha}M}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{k}_{\alpha}}

(2.25)

covering the natural projection
\tau^{k}_{\alpha}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{\alpha}M
by

\left(\mathbf{V}^{k}_{\alpha}\right)_{[\gamma]_{k}}([a]_{\alpha})=[t\mapsto\frac{\alpha!}{k!}t^{k-\alpha}a(t)]_{k}

(2.26)

where
\gamma
is a curve in
M
and
a
is a curve in
E
such that
[\underline{a}]_{k}=[\gamma]_{k}
where
\underline{a}=\sigma\circ a
.

Note that for
\alpha=k-1
we recover the map (
2.5
). It is clear that (
2.26
) does not depend on the choice of representatives
\gamma
and
a
.

The
(\alpha)
-lift
s^{(\alpha)}
of the section
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E)
can be presented as the composition of the complete lift
\mathrm{T}^{\alpha}s
with the vertical lift
{\overrightarrow{\mathbf{V}}}^{k}_{\alpha}
:

s^{(\alpha)}={\overrightarrow{\mathbf{V}}}^{k}_{\alpha}(\mathrm{T}^{\alpha}s).

(2.27)

A simple coordinate-based proof is left to the reader.

A (general) algebroid structure
\kappa
on the vector bundle
\sigma:E\rightarrow M
can be lifted by means of
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-tangent functor to the vector bundle
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma:\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
(see
[
JR18
]
). The lifted structure is called
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order tangent lift of
(\sigma,\kappa)
and denoted as
(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa)
.
The algebroid bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma}
on
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
satisfies

[\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}s_{1}^{(k-\alpha)},\frac{k!}{(k-\beta)!}s_{2}^{(k-\beta)}]_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma}=\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha-\beta)!}([s_{1},s_{2}]_{\sigma})^{(k-\alpha-\beta)}\ ,

(2.28)

for any integers
\alpha,\beta=0,1,\ldots,k
such that
\alpha+\beta\leq k
, and any sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E)
.
Additionally,
[s_{1}^{(k-\alpha)},s_{2}^{(k-\beta)}]_{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma}=0
if
\alpha+\beta>k
. Moreover, if
(\sigma,\kappa)
is a skew/AL/Lie algebroid, then so is
(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{T}^{k}}\kappa)
.

Assume that
(\sigma,\kappa)
is Lie.
From (
2.28
), we observe that assigning the weight
\alpha-\beta
to a section of the form
f^{(\alpha)}s^{(k-\beta)}
, where
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
and
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E)
, turns the Lie subalgebra of
\operatorname{\Gamma}(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma)
generated by homogeneous sections into a graded Lie algebra concentrated in weights
\geq-k
. This Lie algebra has a Lie subalgebra
\operatorname{\Gamma}_{\leq 0}(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma)
generated by homogeneous sections of non-positive weights. It is of finite rank over
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
.

Using the structure of a higher algebroid on a graded bundle
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
one can define
algebroid lifts
of a section
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}_{M}(E^{1})
as follows:

{s}^{\langle{-\alpha}\rangle}:=\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}(s^{(k-\alpha)})\in\mathfrak{X}_{-\alpha}(E^{k}),\hskip 9.24994pt{-k\leq-\alpha\leq 0.}

(2.29)

The notation is slightly different from that in
[
JR18
]
where the algebroid
(k-\alpha)
-lift of a section
s
was
denoted by
{s}^{[k-\alpha]}
and it is related as
{s}^{\langle{-\alpha}\rangle}=\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}{s}^{[k-\alpha]}
.
Thanks to this correction, the vector field
{s}^{\langle{-\alpha}\rangle}
has weight
-\alpha
and the equation (4.6) in
[
JR18
]
simplifies to

[{s_{1}}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle},{s_{2}}^{\langle{{\beta}}\rangle}]={[s_{1},s_{2}]}^{\langle{{\alpha}+{\beta}}\rangle}

(2.30)

for any
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E^{1})
and
{\alpha},{\beta}\leq 0
such that
-k\leq{\alpha}+{\beta}
.

Using (
2.24
) we get

{(fs)}^{\langle{-\alpha}\rangle}=\sum_{\beta=0}^{k-\alpha}\frac{1}{\beta!}(\sharp^{k})^{\ast}f^{(\beta)}\,{s}^{\langle{-\alpha-\beta}\rangle}.

(2.31)

In particular,

{(fs)}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}=f\,{s}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}.

(2.32)

Any vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{0}(E^{k})
of weight
0
has a form

X=X^{a}(x)\partial_{x^{a}}+\sum_{i}X^{i}(x,y)\partial_{y^{i}_{w}},

and has a well defined projection on
M
, denoted by
X\!\!\!\!\downarrow^{k}_{0}=X^{a}(x)\partial_{x^{a}}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. Similarly,
a vector field
Y\in\mathfrak{X}_{-1}(E^{k})
of weight
-1
is projectable onto
E^{1}
, the projection is denoted by
Y\!\!\!\!\downarrow^{k}_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-1}(E^{1})\simeq\operatorname{\Gamma}(E^{1})
, see Lemma
4.1
.
Below is a reformulation of axioms of higher-order algebroids in terms of algebroid lifts.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M
be a graded bundle of order
k
.

(i)

Assume that the order-one reduction of
\sigma^{k}
is a trivial VB of rank
n
, i.e., it admits a trivialization
E^{1}\simeq M\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
let
(e_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n}
be the corresponding frame of
\sigma^{1}:E^{1}\rightarrow M
. A general HA is provided by a graded bundle morphism
\sharp^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
and a collection of homogeneous vector fields
X_{i,{{\alpha}}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{{\alpha}}(E^{k})
, where
-k\leq{\alpha}\leq 0
,
1\leq i\leq n
, such that the projection of each vector field
X_{i,-1}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-1}(E^{k})
onto
E^{1}
coincides with
e_{i}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E^{1})\simeq\mathfrak{X}_{-1}(E^{1})
. Moreover, the vector fields which define
\kappa^{1}
– the order-one reduction of
\kappa^{k}
, are the projections of
X_{i,0}
and
X_{i,-1}
onto
E^{1}
.

(ii)

A skew HA
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
is almost Lie if and only if for any section
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E^{1})
and
-k\leq{\alpha}\leq 0
the vector fields
{s}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{{\alpha}}(E^{k})
and
{\left(\sharp^{1}s\right)}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{{\alpha}}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M)
are
\sharp^{k}
-related.

(iii)

[
JR18
, Proposition 4.9]
An almost Lie HA
(\sigma^{k},\kappa^{k})
is Lie if and only if

{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}|_{\operatorname{\Gamma}_{\leq 0}(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1})}:\operatorname{\Gamma}_{\leq 0}(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1})\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}(E^{k})

is a Lie algebra homomorphism.

Proof.

(i)

The sections
(e_{i}^{({\alpha})})
form a frame for
\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
, hence their pullbacks
\left((\sharp^{k})^{\ast}e_{i}^{({\alpha})}\right)
form a frame for the pullback vector bundle
(\sharp^{k})^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma:\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\times_{(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma,\sharp^{k})}E^{k}\rightarrow E^{k}
. To set a comorphism
\kappa^{k}:\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma{\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}\tau_{E^{k}}
, this amounts to defining a VB morphism from the VB
