(\sharp^{k})^{\ast}\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma
to the tangent bundle of
E^{k}
, covering the identity
\operatorname{id}_{E^{k}}
. This is done by assigning vector fields to the sections from the local frame. We send
(\sharp^{k})^{\ast}e_{i}^{({\alpha})}
to
X_{i,{\alpha}}
. In other words,
{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}(e_{i}^{({\alpha})})=X_{i,{\alpha}}
. Then the obtained comorphism
\kappa^{k}
is weighted, as the vector fields
X_{i,{\alpha}}
are homogeneous and
\sharp^{k}
preserves the weight.

The condition (
2.18
) corresponds to the fact that
e_{i}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E^{1})\simeq\mathfrak{X}_{-1}(E^{1})
coincides with the projection of
X_{i,1}
onto
E^{1}
.

(ii)

The commutativity of the diagram (
2.20
), corresponding to the almost Lie axiom, can be reformulated as follows: For any section
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1})
, the vector fields
{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}(s)\in\mathfrak{X}(E^{k})
and
\kappa^{k}_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}(s)\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M)
are
\sharp^{k}
-related (see the proof of
[
JR18
, Proposition 4.9]
). In particular, in any AL HA
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
, for any section
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E^{1})
and
{\alpha}\geq-k
, the vector fields
{s}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}(E^{k})
and
{(\sharp s)}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M)
are
\sharp^{k}
-related. (The latter are algebroid lifts with respect to the HA structure on
\tau^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
.) On the other hand, if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M)
and
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1})
then

{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}(f\cdot s)=(\sharp^{k})^{\ast}(f)\cdot{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}(s),\hskip 9.24994pt\kappa^{k}_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\sharp^{1}(f\cdot s)=f\cdot\kappa^{k}_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\sharp^{1}(s).

Therefore, if the vector fields
{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}(s)
and
\kappa^{k}_{M}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k}\sharp^{1}(s)
are
\sharp^{k}
-related, then the same is true if we replace
s
with
f\cdot s
. Hence, the thesis (
ii
) holds since sections of the form
s^{(k-{\alpha})}
, where
0\leq{\alpha}\leq k
, span
\operatorname{\Gamma}(\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1})
as
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M)
-module. The proof of (
iii
) is presented in
[
JR18
]
.

∎

Remark 2.12
.

It suffices to verify the conditions given in Theorem
2.11
locally. Moreover, it is sufficient to take the sections of the vector bundle
\sigma^{1}:E^{1}\rightarrow M
to be the elements of a frame
(e_{i})
of local sections. In this way, the almost Lie axiom and Lie axiom can be reduced (locally) to a finite number of equations:

(AL axiom)

The vector fields
{e_{i}}^{\langle{-{\alpha}}\rangle}
and
{(\sharp e_{i})}^{\langle{-{\alpha}}\rangle}
are
\sharp^{k}
-related for any
0\leq{\alpha}\leq k
.

(Lie axiom)

[{e_{i}}^{\langle{-{\alpha}}\rangle},{e_{j}}^{\langle{-\beta}\rangle}]_{\tau_{E^{k}}}={[e_{i},e_{j}]_{\sigma^{1}}}^{\langle{-{\alpha}-\beta}\rangle}
for any
0\leq{\alpha},\beta
such that
\alpha+\beta\leq k
.

Remark 2.13
.

There is also a dual construction of the algebroid lifts
{s}^{\langle{-{\alpha}}\rangle}
associated with a HA
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
, which coincides with the construction presented in
[
GU99
]
for Lie algebroids, i.e., when
k=1
. Given a section
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E)
considered as a linear function
\iota(s)
on
E^{\ast}
, we have
({\alpha})
-lifts
\iota(s)^{({\alpha})}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast})
for
{\alpha}=0,1,\ldots,k
. As we mentioned (see (
2.22
)), the vector bundles
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma:\mathrm{T}^{k}E\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
and
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{\ast}:\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
are in natural duality, hence the dual of
\kappa^{k}
is a weighted vector bundle morphism
\varepsilon^{k}
of the form

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\ast}_{E^{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{\ast}}
\textstyle{E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}

By pulling back
\iota(s)^{({\alpha})}
via
\varepsilon^{k}
we obtain linear functions on
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}E^{k}
, thus vector fields on
E^{k}
.
It is evident (by working fiberwise) that this way we recover our algebroid lifts, i.e,

\langle{s}^{\langle{-{\alpha}}\rangle},\cdot\rangle_{\tau_{E^{k}}}=(\varepsilon^{k})^{\ast}\iota(s)^{(k-{\alpha})}

Let
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
be a HA and
(E^{j},\kappa^{j})
be its reduction to order
j
, where
1\leq j<k
. The following lemma states that algebroid lifts
{s}^{\langle\alpha\rangle_{\kappa^{k}}}
and
{s}^{\langle\alpha\rangle_{\kappa^{j}}}
obtained using
\kappa^{k}
and
\kappa^{j}
, respectively, are compatible in some natural sense.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(E^{1})
and
0\leq\alpha\leq j<k
. Then the vector field
{s}^{\langle{-}\alpha\rangle_{\kappa^{k}}}\in\mathfrak{X}(E^{k})
is projectable onto
E^{j}
and its projection is
{s}^{\langle-\alpha\rangle_{\kappa^{j}}}
.

Proof.

We shall use the construction of
({\alpha})
-lifts of a section
s
, as defined in (
2.27
). We have

{s}^{\langle{-\alpha}\rangle_{\kappa^{k}}}=\frac{k!}{(k-\alpha)!}\,{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}\left({\overrightarrow{\mathbf{V}}}^{k}_{k-\alpha}(\mathrm{T}^{k-\alpha}s)\right)={\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}(\xi^{k}_{\alpha}),

where the section
\xi^{k}_{\alpha}:\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}
is defined by
[\gamma]_{k}\mapsto[t\mapsto t^{\alpha}\cdot s(\gamma(t))]_{k}
where
\gamma
is a curve in
M
.
Hence, the vector field
{s}^{\langle{-\alpha}\rangle_{\kappa^{k}}}
is the composition of maps
\operatorname{id}_{E^{k}}\times(\xi^{k}_{\alpha}\circ\sharp^{k}):E^{k}\rightarrow E^{k}\times_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}
with the VB morphism
(\kappa^{k})^{!}:E^{k}\times_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}E^{k}
induced by
\kappa^{k}
. The thesis follows from the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{k}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}_{E^{k}}\times(\xi^{k}_{\alpha}\circ\sharp^{k})}
\textstyle{E^{k}\times_{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\mathrm{T}^{k}E^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\kappa^{k})^{!}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\sigma^{k}_{j}}
\textstyle{E^{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}_{E^{j}}\times(\xi^{j}_{\alpha}\circ\sharp^{j})}
\textstyle{E^{j}\times_{\mathrm{T}^{j}M}\mathrm{T}^{j}E^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\kappa^{j})^{!}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{j}}

∎

2.8
Prolongations of an almost Lie algebroid

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid with source and target maps denoted by
\alpha,\beta:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow M
, respectively. We consider a foliation
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
on
\mathcal{G}
defined by
\alpha
-fibers
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\alpha}=\{g\in\mathcal{G}:\alpha(g)=x\}
, the distribution
\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\subset\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
tangent to the leaves of
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
, related objects like
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
and the right action of
\mathcal{G}
on itself,
R_{g}:h\mapsto hg
where
h\in\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\beta(g)}
.
The Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}
is usually defined as the vector bundle
\sigma:\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G}):=\mathrm{T}_{M}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\rightarrow M
equipped with a map
\sharp:\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
called the anchor, defined as
\sharp=\mathrm{T}\beta|_{\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})}
and the Lie bracket on
\operatorname{\Gamma}(\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G}))
inherited from the Lie bracket of right-invariant vector fields on
\mathcal{G}
. (Such vector fields are in a one-to-one correspondence with sections of
\sigma
.) Another, yet equivalent construction of the Lie algebroid structure on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})
, is provided by the
reduction map

\mathcal{R}^{1}
,

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{R}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{\mathcal{G}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{M,}

(2.33)

which is a fiber-wise VB isomorphism obtained from the collection of maps
\mathrm{T}R_{g^{-1}}:\mathrm{T}_{g}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}_{\beta(g)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
. The Lie algebroid structure on
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})
is defined by means of the VB comorphism
\kappa:\mathrm{T}\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G}){\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{G})
which is obtained as the reduction of
\kappa_{\mathcal{G}}:\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}
. The advantage of the latter over the standard construction of the Lie functor is that it can be easily generalized to higher orders. This is obtained by means of the higher-order reduction map
\mathcal{R}^{k}:\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}_{M}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
, defined analogously to
\mathcal{R}^{1}
, by the collection of maps
\mathrm{T}^{k}R_{g^{-1}}:\mathrm{T}^{k}_{g}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}_{\beta(g)}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
.

Definition 2.15
.

[
JR15
, Definition 3.3, Lemma 3.4]
The
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order Lie algebroid of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is the graded bundle
\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G}):=\mathrm{T}^{k}_{M}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
together with a VB comorphism
\kappa^{k}:=(\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{R}^{1},\mathrm{T}\mathcal{R}^{k})(\widetilde{\kappa}^{k}_{\mathcal{G}})
where
\widetilde{\kappa}^{k}_{\mathcal{G}}
is the restriction of
\kappa^{k}_{\mathcal{G}}
to
(\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T})\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\times(\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k})\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}
subject to the natural inclusions
(\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T})\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}(\mathrm{T}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha})
and
(\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k})\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}\subset\mathrm{T}(\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha})
.

Actually,
(\mathcal{A}^{k}(\mathcal{G}),\kappa^{k})
is a Lie HA in the sense of Definition
2.7
(
[
JR18
, Proposition 4.13]
and
[
JR15
, Section 5]
).

In
[
JR15
]
we introduced a slightly bigger class of examples of HAs obtained by means of the construction called
the prolongation
of an almost Lie algebroid
(A,\kappa)
. We will outline this construction, highlighting a possible more general context for certain constructions.

A pair of a vector bundle
\sigma:A\rightarrow M
and a VB morphism
\sharp:A\to\mathrm{T}M
covering the identity
\operatorname{id}_{M}
is called an
anchored vector bundle
. A curve
a:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow A
is called
admissible
if the tangent lift of the curve
\underline{a}=\sigma\circ a:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow M
coincides with the curve
\sharp\circ a
, i.e.,

\bm{\mathrm{t}}\underline{a}=\sharp\circ a
. The subset
A^{[k]}
of
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
, defined as

A^{[k]}=\{[a]_{k-1}\mid a:\mathbb{R}\to A\text{ is an admissible curve }\},

(2.34)

is called the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order prolongation of the anchored vector bundle
A
(see
[
Pop04
]
). According to
[
BGG15b
, Theorem 2.2.7]
, we have

A^{[2]}=\{X\in\mathrm{T}A:(\mathrm{T}\sigma)X=\sharp\,\tau_{A}(X)\},

(2.35)

and

A^{[k]}=(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sharp)^{-1}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M)

where
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
is considered as a subset of
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}M
via
i^{k-1,1}_{M}
.
It follows that the constructions of the sets
A^{[k]}
here and
E^{k}
in
[
JR15
, Definition 4.1]
are equivalent, see also
[
BGG15b
]
or
[
JR15
, Theorem 4.5 (viii)]
, or
[
Mar15
]
. In particular,
A^{[k+1]}=\mathrm{T}A^{[k]}\cap\mathrm{T}^{k}A
, considered as subsets of
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
.

Define
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order anchor map
\sharp^{[k]}:A^{[k]}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
as
\sharp^{[k]}=(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sharp)|_{A^{[k]}}
. It is a graded bundle morphism.

An AL algebroid structure on the vector bundle
A
can be prolonged to a HA structure on
A^{[k]}
by means of the comorphisms
\kappa^{[k]}:\mathrm{T}^{k}A{\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}\mathrm{T}A^{[k]}
, covering
\sharp^{[k]}
, defined as

\kappa^{[k]}=(\kappa_{A}^{k-1}\circ\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa)\cap(\mathrm{T}^{k}A\cap\mathrm{T}A^{[k]}),

(2.36)

see
[
JR15
, Proposition 4.6]
. The comorphism
\kappa^{[k]}
can also be defined inductively as it is presented in
[
JR15
, Definition 4.2]
.

2.9
Canonical inclusions II

Here, we highlight some natural embeddings induced by the anchored bundle structure on a vector bundle
A\rightarrow M
.

In addition to the inclusion
A^{[k]}\subseteq\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
from the definition of
A^{[k]}
(
2.34
), there are inclusions
\imath^{k,l}_{A}:A^{[k+l]}\to\mathrm{T}^{k}A^{[l]}
defined by the restriction of
i^{k,l-1}_{A}
to
A^{[k+l]}
:

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k+l-1}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{k,l-1}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}^{l-1}A}
\textstyle{A^{[k+l]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\imath^{k,l}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}A^{[l]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We should prove that the image
\imath^{k,l}_{A}(A^{[k+l]})
is in
\mathrm{T}^{k}A^{[l]}
, considered as a subset of
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}^{l-1}A
. Let
[a]_{k+l-1}\in A^{[k+l]}
where
a
is an admissible path in
A
. Then
\imath^{k,l}_{A}([a]_{k+l-1})=\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{k}_{t=0}\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{l-1}_{s=0}a(t+s)
. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the path
s\mapsto a(t+s)
is admissible, hence the curve
t\mapsto\bm{\mathrm{t}}^{l-1}_{s=0}a(t+s)
lies in
A^{[l-1]}
, so
\imath^{k,l}_{A}([a]_{k+l-1})\in\mathrm{T}^{k}A^{[l-1]}
as we claimed. Using (
2.10
) we find that

(\imath^{k,l}_{A})^{\ast}(y^{i,({\alpha},{\beta})})=y^{i,({\alpha}+{\beta})}

(2.37)

where
\alpha\leq k
,
{\beta}\leq l-1
. Recall,
(x^{a},y^{i,({\alpha})})
,
0\leq{\alpha}\leq k-1
is a coordinate chart for
A^{[k]}
induced from
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
.

The rank of the graded bundle
A^{[k]}
is
(r,r,r,\ldots,r)
where
r=\operatorname{rank}A
. Since
\imath^{k-1,1}_{A}
is an inclusion and the ranks of the VBs
\widehat{A^{[k]}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A}
are the same, it induces an isomorphism
\widehat{\imath^{k-1,1}_{A}}:\widehat{A^{[k]}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A}
of the core bundles. We define an isomorphism
\jmath^{[k]}_{A}:A\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widehat{A^{[k]}}\subset A^{[k]}
using the diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\jmath^{[k]}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\jmath^{k}_{A}}
\textstyle{\widehat{A^{[k]}}\subset A^{[k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\imath^{k-1,1}_{A}}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A.}

(2.38)

i.e.,
\imath^{k-1,1}_{A}\circ\jmath^{[k]}_{A}:A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
coincides with
\jmath^{k}_{A}:A\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
. In the special case
A=\mathrm{T}M
, the map
\jmath^{[k]}_{\mathrm{T}M}
coincides with
j^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}M\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}
, due to (
2.12
).

The following statement concerns the structure of the prolongation of an AL algebroid:

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
(A,\kappa)
be an AL algebroid. The following diagram of isomorphisms is commutative

\textstyle{\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\jmath^{[k]}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{s\mapsto\frac{1}{k!}{s}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{A^{[k]}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v\mapsto{v^{\uparrow}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{X}_{-k}(A^{[k]}).}

In particular, for
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathfrak{X}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{k}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{X\mapsto{\frac{1}{k!}}{X}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k}M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{X}_{-k}(\mathrm{T}^{k}M),}

where
{X}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}
is the algebroid
(-k)
-lift of the vector field
X
, associated with the HA
(\mathrm{T}^{k}M,\kappa^{k}_{M})
. Moreover, the core of the anchor map
\sharp^{[k]}:A^{[k]}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
can be identified with
\sharp
under the isomorphisms
\jmath^{[k]}_{A}:A\rightarrow\widehat{A^{[k]}}
and
j^{k}_{M}:\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}
.

Proof.

In view of (
2.32
), it suffices to check that the first diagram is commutative for sections from the local frame
(e_{i})
of
\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
. Due to the definition of
\jmath^{[k]}_{A}
, this problem reduces to verifying that the vector fields
\frac{1}{k!}{s}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-k}(A^{[k]})
and
{\left(\jmath^{k}_{A}(s)\right)^{\uparrow}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-k}(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A)
are
\imath^{k-1,1}_{A}
-related, where we can take
s=e_{i}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
. From (
2.11
), we see that
{\left(\jmath^{k}_{A}(e_{i})\right)^{\uparrow}}=\partial_{y^{i,(k-1)}}
. The vector field
{s}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}
denotes the algebroid lift of
s
with respect to
(A^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]})
, the
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order prolongation of the algebroid
(A,\kappa)
. From the definition of algebroid lifts and (
2.23
) we obtain

\frac{1}{k!}{e_{i}}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}={\overrightarrow{\kappa^{[k]}}}(e_{i}^{(0)})={\overrightarrow{\kappa^{[k]}}}({\overrightarrow{\mathbf{V}^{k}_{0}}}e_{i})=\partial_{y^{i,(k-1)}},

The last equality follows from the fact that
\kappa
is the identity on the core bundle; hence, the same holds for
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\kappa
, as well as for
\kappa_{A}^{k-1}:\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}A\to\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
and
\kappa^{[k]}
.

For the last statement, concerning the case
A=\mathrm{T}M
, note that the inclusions
A^{[k]}\to\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
and
\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}M
induce the identity on the cores.
Hence,
\widehat{\sharp^{[k]}}
coincides with
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sharp}
, which can be identified with
\sharp:A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
, as claimed.
∎

3
Structure of higher algebroids

In this section
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
is a HA of order
k
and
(A=E^{1},\kappa=\kappa^{1})
is its reduction to order one.

3.1
Morphism
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}:A^{[k]}\rightarrow E^{k}
.

We shall construct a canonical VB morphism from
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order prolongation
A^{[k]}
of an AL algebroid
A
(see Preliminaries) to a given
k^{\mathrm{th}}
-order HA
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
whose order-one reduction coincides with
A
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
be a HA of order
k
. We apply the functor
\lambda
to the relation
\kappa^{k}\in\mathcal{GB}[k,1]
and define the relation
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
to be the intersection of
A^{[k]}\times E^{k}
with
\lambda(\kappa^{k})
subject to the natural inclusions and isomorphisms:
\imath^{k-1,1}_{A}:A^{[k]}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A\simeq\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}A)
, and
\mathrm{diag}^{k}:E^{k}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})=\lambda(\mathrm{T}E^{k})
(defined in Preliminaries):

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A\simeq\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(\kappa^{k})}
\textstyle{\lambda(\mathrm{T}E^{k}){=}{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})}
\textstyle{A^{[k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}}
\textstyle{E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
(\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k})
be an AL HA and let
(A,\kappa)
be its order-one reduction. Then

(a)

\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
is (the graph of) a
\mathcal{GB}[k]
-morphism,
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}:A^{[k]}\rightarrow E^{k}
,

(b)

\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
intertwines the anchor morphisms:
\sharp^{k}\circ\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}=\sharp^{[k]}
.

Proof.

First, we shall prove that if
(U,V)\in\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
then
\sharp^{[k]}(U)=\sharp^{k}(V)
. Then we shall show that
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
is a mapping, and this will complete the proof of (
a
) and (
b
). Indeed, in vie of the characterisation of graded bundle morphisms
[
GR11
]
, we only need to add that the relation
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
is invariant with respect to the homogeneity structure on
A^{[k]}\times E^{k}
. This is because the inclusions
A^{[k]}\hookrightarrow\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}A)
,
E^{k}\hookrightarrow\lambda(\mathrm{T}E^{k})
are graded bundle morphisms.

Take
(U,V)\in\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
, let us denote by
\tilde{U}
(resp.,
\tilde{V}
) the images of
U
(resp.
V
) in
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
(resp.,
{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
) and consider the diagram

\textstyle{\tilde{U}\in\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sharp^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(\kappa^{k})}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})\ni\tilde{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(\sharp^{k})}
\textstyle{U\in A^{[k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{[k]}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}}
\textstyle{E^{k}\ni V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{M}^{k-1}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{k-1,1}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{1,k-1}_{M}}

•

The top trapezoid in the middle commutes (i.e.,
(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sharp^{1},{\operatorname{lin}}(\sharp^{k})):\lambda(\kappa^{k})\Rightarrow\kappa_{M}^{k-1}
is a morphism in the category
\mathcal{VBC}
) because it is obtained by applying the functor
\lambda
to the diagram (
2.20
), which is commutative since
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
is almost Lie. Here we used Lemmas
2.2
and
2.5
.

•

The parallelogram on the left also commutes as
\sharp^{[k]}=\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sharp^{1}|_{A^{[k]}}
, see
[
JR15
, Theorem 4.5 (ix)]
.

•

The parallelogram on the right also commutes. This follows from a more general
Lemma
2.3
.

As
(U,V)\in\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
, so
(\tilde{U},\tilde{V})\in\lambda(\kappa^{k})
, hence
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sharp^{1}(\tilde{U})\in\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}M
and
{\operatorname{lin}}(\sharp^{k})(\tilde{V})\in\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
are related by means of
\kappa_{M}^{k-1}
. However, due to the commutativity of the left and right parallelograms, both
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sharp^{1}(\tilde{U})
and
{\operatorname{lin}}(\sharp^{k})(\tilde{V})
are images of
\sharp^{[k]}(U)
and
\sharp^{k}(V)
, respectively, under the canonical inclusions of
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
into
\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathrm{T}M
and
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
, respectively. Moreover, these images are
\kappa_{M}^{k-1}
-related, due to the commutativity of the top trapezoid. Since
\kappa^{k-1}_{M}
intertwines the canonical inclusions,
\kappa_{M}^{k-1}\circ i^{k-1,1}_{M}=i^{1,k-1}_{M}
,
we get
\sharp^{[k]}(U)=\sharp^{k}(V)
, as was claimed.

Now we shall prove (
a
), i.e., that the relation
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
is a mapping. We shall proceed by induction on
k
.

Obviously,
\mathrm{\Theta}^{1}:A\rightarrow A
is the identity mapping.
Let
k>1
and assume that
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k-1}:A^{[k-1]}\rightarrow E^{k-1}
is a mapping. The graph of
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k-1}
is invariant with respect to the homogeneity structure of
A^{[k-1]}\times E^{k-1}
, hence
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k-1}
is a morphism of graded bundles.

Step A.
We shall fist prove that for any
U^{k}\in A^{[k]}
there is at least one
V^{k}\in E^{k}
such that
(U^{k},V^{k})\in\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
.

We know from Lemma
2.5
that
\lambda(\kappa^{k})
is a VB comorphism covering
\sharp^{k-1}:E^{k-1}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
. Set
\tilde{V}^{k}=\lambda(\kappa^{k})_{v}(\tilde{U}^{k})\in{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
, where
v:=\mathrm{\Theta}^{k-1}(U^{k-1})\in E^{k-1}
and
U^{k-1}=\sigma^{k}_{k-1}(U^{k})\in A^{[k-1]}
. Consider the diagram

\textstyle{\tilde{U}^{k}\in\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sigma^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(\kappa^{k})}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})\ni\tilde{V}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U^{k}\in A^{[k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowleft}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}}
\textstyle{E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M}
\textstyle{A^{[k-1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{[k-1]}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{\Theta}^{k-1}}
\textstyle{E^{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{E^{k-1}\ni v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{k-1}}

We shall check first that the definition of
\tilde{V}^{k}
is correct, i.e.,

\sharp^{k-1}(v)=\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sigma^{1}(\tilde{U}^{k}).

(3.1)

This amounts to show that the compositions
A^{[k]}\to A^{[k-1]}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\Theta}^{k-1}}E^{k-1}\xrightarrow{\sharp^{k-1}}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
and
A^{[k]}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A\to\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
coincide.
According to our inductive hypothesis,
\sharp^{k-1}\circ\mathrm{\Theta}^{k-1}
is equal to
\sharp^{[k-1]}
, hence (
3.1
) reduces to the commutativity of the square diagram on the left (pointed by the circular arrow
\circlearrowleft
).
The map
\sharp^{[k-1]}
is the restriction of
\mathrm{T}^{k-2}\sharp
to
A^{[k-1]}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-2}A
, see
[
JR15
, Theorem 4.5 (ix)]
, hence it suffices to prove that the following diagram is commutative.

\textstyle{A^{[k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{[k-1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-2}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-2}\sharp}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sigma}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-2}\mathrm{T}M}

The inclusions
A^{[k]}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
are compatible with projections
\mathrm{T}^{k+1}A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}A
, i.e., the following diagram on the left is commutative:

\textstyle{A^{[k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{[k-1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-2}A,}

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sigma}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-2}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-2}\sharp}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k-2}\mathrm{T}M}

The diagram on the right is not commutative in general, however for
X\in A^{[k]}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
we have (by
[
JR15
, Theorem 4.5 (viii)]
):

i^{k-2,1}_{M}\circ(\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\sigma)(X)=(\mathrm{T}^{k-2}\sharp)\circ\tau^{k-1}_{k-2}(X)
. This is enough for our claim (
3.1
).

Now we prove that
\tilde{V}^{k}\in{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
is in
E^{k}\subset{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
. Consider the diagram

\textstyle{E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{diag}^{k}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})\ni\tilde{V}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(\sigma^{k}_{k-1})}
\textstyle{v\in E^{k-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{diag}^{k-1}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k-1})}

The subset of
{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
of those elements
X
for which
{\operatorname{lin}}(\sigma^{k}_{k-1})(X)=\mathrm{diag}^{k-1}(\pi(X))
coincides with
E^{k}
. We are given
v=\mathrm{\Theta}^{k-1}(U^{k-1})\in E^{k-1}
and
\tilde{V}^{k}\in{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
such that
\pi(\tilde{V}^{k})=v
and
\mathrm{diag}^{k-1}(v)={\operatorname{lin}}(\sigma^{k}_{k-1})(\tilde{V}^{k})
. It follows that
\tilde{V}^{k}\in E^{k}
.

Step B.
We shall prove that for a given
U^{k}\in A^{[k]}
there is at most one
V^{k}\in E^{k}
such that
(U^{k},V^{k})\in\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
. This will finish the proof of (
a
) and (
b
).

Assume
(U^{k},V^{k}_{i})
are in
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
for
i=1,2
. Using the inductive hypothesis, we know that
V^{k-1}_{1}=V^{k-1}_{2}\in E^{k-1}
, hence
V^{k}_{i}
, considered as elements of
{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})
, are in the same fiber of the vector bundle
\pi:{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})\rightarrow E^{k-1}
. As
(U^{k},V^{k}_{i})\in\lambda(\kappa^{k})
and
\lambda(\kappa^{k})
is a VB comorphism over
\sharp^{k-1}:E^{k-1}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M
, we must have
V^{k}_{1}=\lambda(\kappa^{k})_{v}(U^{k})=V^{k}_{2}
as we claimed.
∎

Example 3.3

(
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
and
\mathrm{\Theta}^{3}
in coordinates)
.

We shall provide an explicit coordinate expression for
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}:A^{[2]}\rightarrow E^{2}
, assuming
\kappa^{2}
is given a general local form as in (
2.21
). According to the procedure given in the Definition
2.1
, to get
\lambda(\kappa^{2})
, in the first step we set
y^{i}=0
and
\underline{x}^{a}=0
. Then we eliminate coordinates of weight
(2,0)
, i.e., the coordinates
\ddot{x}^{a}
and
\underline{z}^{\mu}
. In this way we arrive at the VB comorphism
\lambda(\kappa^{2}):\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{2}A){\rightarrow\!\!\vartriangleright}{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{2})
over
\sharp:A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
given by

\lambda(\kappa^{2}):\begin{cases}\dot{x}^{a}=&Q^{a}_{i}\,\underline{y}^{i},\\
\dot{\underline{y}}^{i}=&\dot{y}^{i},\\
\dot{\underline{z}}^{\mu}=&Q^{\mu}_{i}\,\ddot{y}^{i}+Q^{\mu}_{ij}\,\underline{y}^{i}\dot{y}^{j}.\end{cases}

(3.2)

Let
(U^{2},V^{2})\in A^{[2]}\times E^{2}
, and let
(\tilde{U}^{2},\tilde{V}^{2})\in\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{2}A)\times{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{2})
be the image of
(U^{2},V^{2})
under
the canonical inclusions (see (
2.37
), (
2.6
), (
2.13
)):

I^{2}_{E}\circ\imath^{1,1}_{A}:A^{[2]}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathrm{T}A\simeq\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{2}A),\hskip 9.24994pt(x^{a},\dot{x}^{a},\dot{y}^{i},\ddot{y}^{i})(\tilde{U}^{2})=(x^{a},Q^{a}_{i}y^{i},y^{i},2\dot{y}^{i})(U^{2})

and

\mathrm{diag}^{2}:E^{2}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{2}),\hskip 9.24994pt(\underline{x}^{a},\underline{y}^{i},\underline{\dot{y}}^{i},\underline{\dot{z}}^{\mu})(\tilde{V}^{2})=(\underline{x}^{a},\underline{y}^{i},\underline{y}^{i},2\underline{z}^{\mu})(V^{2}).

(Recall that
(x^{a,({\alpha})},y^{i,({\beta})})
,
0\leq{\alpha}\leq k-1
,
1\leq{\beta}\leq k
, are coordinates for
\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{k}A)
inherited from the adapted coordinates on
\mathrm{T}^{k}A
. The coordinate system for
{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{k})=\lambda(\mathrm{T}E^{k})
is inherited from the adapted coordinate system on the tangent bundle of
E^{k}
.)
By plugging these expressions to (
3.2
) we find that

(\tilde{U}^{2},\tilde{V}^{2})\in\lambda(\kappa^{2})
if and only if

\begin{cases}Q^{a}_{i}y^{i}&=Q^{a}_{i}\,\underline{y}^{i},\\
\underline{y}^{i}&=y^{i},\\
2\underline{z}^{\mu}&=Q^{\mu}_{i}\,2\dot{y}^{i}+Q^{\mu}_{ij}\,\underline{y}^{i}y^{j},\end{cases}

hence

\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}

is an affine bundle morphism
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}:A^{[2]}\rightarrow E^{2}
covering the identity
\operatorname{id}_{A}
given by

\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}(x^{a},y^{i},\dot{y}^{i})=(\underline{x}^{a}=x^{a},\underline{y}^{i}=y^{i},\underline{z}^{\mu}=Q^{\mu}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}y^{i}y^{j}),

(3.3)

where

Q^{\mu}_{ij}=Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}+Q^{\mu}_{[ij]}
is the decomposition into symmetric and anty-symmetric part, namely

Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}=\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{ji},\hskip 9.24994ptQ^{\mu}_{[ij]}=\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{ji}.

(3.4)

In order three, additional equations for
\kappa^{3}
appear. Let
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu},t^{\alpha})
be graded coordinates on a graded bundle
\sigma^{3}:E^{3}\rightarrow M
where the coordinates
t^{\alpha}
have order 3. The additional equations for
\kappa^{3}
, extending those for
\kappa^{2}
, are of the form (we have omitted expressions that do not account for
\lambda(\kappa^{3})
):
\dddot{x}^{a}=\dddot{x}^{a}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{t})
and

\dot{\underline{t}}^{\alpha}=Q^{\alpha}_{i}\dddot{y}^{i}+Q^{\alpha}_{ij}\underline{y}^{i}\ddot{y}^{j}+Q^{\alpha}_{\nu i}\underline{z}^{\nu}\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\alpha}_{ij,k}\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}\dot{y}^{k}+{q^{\alpha}_{i}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{t})y^{i}}

for some functions
q^{\alpha}_{i}
on
E^{3}
of weight
3
.
Now we set to zero the coordinates of weight
(0,1)
, i.e.,
y^{i}=0
,
\underline{\dot{x}}^{a}=0
and eliminate the coordinates of weight
(3,0)
,
\dddot{x}^{a}
and
\underline{t}^{\alpha}
. This way we get, in addition to (
3.2
), the following equations defining
\lambda(\kappa^{3})
:

\lambda(\kappa^{3}):\begin{cases}\ddot{x}^{a}&=Q^{a}_{\mu}\underline{z}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{a}_{ij}\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j},\\
\dot{\underline{t}}^{\alpha}&=Q^{\alpha}_{i}\dddot{y}^{i}+Q^{\alpha}_{ij}\underline{y}^{i}\ddot{y}^{j}+Q^{\alpha}_{\nu i}\underline{z}^{\nu}\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\alpha}_{ij,k}\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}\dot{y}^{k}.\end{cases}

(3.5)

which is a VB comorphism over
\sharp^{2}
:

\textstyle{A^{[3]}\subset\mathrm{T}^{2}A\simeq\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{3}A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(\kappa^{3})}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{3})\supset E^{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}
\textstyle{E^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{2}}

Let
U^{3}\in A^{[3]}
,
V^{3}\in E^{3}
and let
\tilde{U}^{3}
in
\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{3}A)
and
\tilde{V}^{3}
in
{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{3})
denote their images subject to the inclusions
A^{[3]}\xhookrightarrow{}\lambda(\mathrm{T}^{2}A)
and
\mathrm{diag}:E^{3}\xhookrightarrow{}{\operatorname{lin}}(E^{3})
. We have

\begin{split}(x^{a},\dot{x}^{a},\ddot{x}^{a};\dot{y}^{i},\ddot{y}^{i},\dddot{y}^{i})(\tilde{U}^{3})=(x^{a},Q^{a}_{i}y^{i},X^{a};y^{i},2\dot{y}^{i},3\ddot{y}^{i})(U^{3})\\
(x^{a},\underline{y}^{i},\underline{z}^{\mu};\underline{\dot{y}}^{i},\underline{\dot{z}}^{\mu},\underline{\dot{t}}^{\alpha})(\tilde{V}^{3})=(x^{a},\underline{y}^{i},\underline{z}^{\mu},\underline{y}^{i},2\underline{z}^{\mu},3\underline{t}^{\alpha})(V^{3}).\end{split}

(3.6)

where, due to the definition of
A^{[k]}
,

X^{a}=\ddot{x}^{a}(\tilde{U}^{3})=(Q^{a}_{i}y^{i})^{\cdot}=\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}\dot{x}^{b}y^{i}+Q^{a}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}y^{i}y^{j}+Q^{a}_{i}\dot{y}^{i},

where

\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}=\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}Q^{b}_{j}+\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{j}}{\partial x^{b}}Q^{b}_{i}.

(3.7)

Now assume that
\tilde{U}^{3}
,
\tilde{V}^{3}
are
\lambda(\kappa^{3})
-related. We shall show that the first equation for
\lambda(\kappa^{3})
in (
3.5
)
is satisfied automatically. It amounts to show that
\ddot{x}^{a}(\tilde{U}^{3})
given above coincides with

Q^{a}_{\mu}\underline{z}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{a}_{ij}\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}(\text{on }\tilde{V}^{3})=Q^{a}_{\mu}\left(Q^{\mu}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}y^{i}y^{j}\right)+\frac{1}{2}Q^{a}_{ij}y^{i}y^{j}(\text{on }\tilde{U}^{3}).

The last equality is due to (
3.3
) as the reductions of
\tilde{U}^{3}
,
\tilde{V}^{3}
to order 2 are
\lambda(\kappa^{2})
-related. By comparing the coefficients at
\dot{y}^{i}
and at
y^{i}y^{j}
we see that the first equation in (
3.5
) is equivalent to the equations (
4.13a
) and (
4.16b
) considered in Appendix, which are true in any order-two AL HA.
The second equation for
\lambda(\kappa^{3})
gives

\underline{t}^{\alpha}=\underline{t}^{\alpha}(V^{3})=\frac{1}{3}\underline{\dot{t}}^{\alpha}(\tilde{V}^{3})=\frac{1}{3}Q^{\alpha}_{i}\dddot{y}^{i}(\tilde{U}^{3})+\frac{1}{3}Q^{\alpha}_{ij}\underline{y}^{i}(\tilde{V}^{3})\ddot{y}^{j}(\tilde{U}^{3})+\frac{1}{3}Q^{\alpha}_{\nu i}\underline{z}^{\nu}(\tilde{V}^{3})\dot{y}^{i}(\tilde{U}^{3})+\frac{1}{6}Q^{\alpha}_{ij,k}\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}(\tilde{V}^{3})\dot{y}^{k}(\tilde{U}^{3})

from which, using (
3.6
), we find a complete formula for
\mathrm{\Theta}^{3}
,

\begin{split}\mathrm{\Theta}^{3}:(x^{a},y^{i},\dot{y}^{i},\ddot{y}^{i})\mapsto(\underline{x}^{a}=x^{a},\underline{y}^{i}=y^{i},\underline{z}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}y^{i}y^{j},\\
\underline{t}^{\alpha}=Q^{\alpha}_{i}\ddot{y}^{i}+(\frac{2}{3}Q^{\alpha}_{ij}+\frac{1}{3}Q^{\alpha}_{\nu i}Q^{\nu}_{j})y^{i}\dot{y}^{j}+\frac{1}{6}(Q^{\alpha}_{\nu i}Q^{\nu}_{jk}+Q^{\alpha}_{ij,k})y^{i}y^{j}y^{k}).\end{split}

(3.8)

Remark 3.4
.

In deriving the formula for the mapping
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
we did not use the assumption from Theorem
3.2
that
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
is AL. Actually,
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
is a well defined mapping for any skew HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
.

Conjecture 3.5
.

Let
(\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k})
be a Lie HA. Then
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}:A^{[k]}\rightarrow E^{k}
is a HA morphism.

It suffices to prove that
(\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{\Theta}^{1},\mathrm{T}\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}):\kappa^{[k]}\Rightarrow\kappa^{k}
is a
\mathcal{VBC}
-morphism. Recall,
\mathrm{\Theta}^{1}
is the identity on
E^{1}
, hence it remains to verify that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{[k]}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}A^{[k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}
\textstyle{E^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{k}}
\textstyle{A^{[k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sharp^{[k]}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}}

(3.9)

is commutative. We already know that the bottom triangle is commutative (see Theorem
3.2
). Therefore, we now need to prove that

\left(\kappa^{k}\right)_{\underline{Z}}(X)=\mathrm{T}\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}(\left(\kappa^{k}\right)_{\underline{Y}}(X))

(3.10)

for any
\underline{Y}\in A^{[k]}
and
X\in\mathrm{T}^{k}A
such that
\mathrm{T}^{k}\sigma^{1}(X)=\sharp^{[k]}(\underline{Y})
, where
\underline{Z}=\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}(\underline{Y})\in E^{k}
. In other words, this means that the algebroid
\langle{\alpha}\rangle
-lifts with respect to
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
and
(A^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]})
are
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}
related for all
-k\leq\alpha\leq 0
.

We shall prove Conjecture
3.5
for
k=2
by direct computations. See Appendix, Subsection
4.3
.

3.2
Higher algebroids in order two

In this subsection, we shall look closer at higher algebroids
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
of order two. First, we shall describe the structure of the graded bundle
E^{2}
, see Lemma
3.6
. Then, we
shall derive a number of
structure maps
which fully determine
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
and reformulate the definition of a skew HA in terms of these structure maps and relations between them, see Theorem
3.13
. We shall examine skew and Lie HAs in which the base
M
is a point, see Theorem
3.15
.
We shall also find the relations between the structure maps and the conditions under which
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
becomes an almost Lie (Theorem
3.16
) and a Lie HA (Theorem
3.20
). Finally, we will describe the relation between order-two HAs and Lie algebroid representations up to homotopy, see Theorem
3.26
.
We assume that
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
is a
skew
HA.

Throughout this subsection, we denote by
A
the order-one reduction of
E^{2}
, i.e.,
A=E^{1}
, and
C=\widehat{E^{2}}
– the core of
E^{2}
. We set
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu})
as a system of graded coordinates on
E^{2}
and fix local frames
(e_{i})
of
\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
and
(c_{\mu})
of
\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
such that

y^{i}(e_{j})=\delta^{i}_{j},\hskip 9.24994ptz^{\mu}|_{C}(c_{\nu})=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}.

(3.11)

3.2.1
The structure of the graded bundle of a skew HA of order two

Define

\partial:=\widehat{\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}}:A\rightarrow C

(3.12)

as the core of the map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}:A^{[2]}\rightarrow E^{2}
, see Definition
3.1
, where the core of
A^{[2]}
is identified with
A
under the isomorphism
\jmath^{[2]}_{A}:A\simeq\widehat{A^{[2]}}
, see (
2.38
). .
Consider the map
\tilde{\mathrm{\Theta}}^{2}
defined on the product (over
M
) of graded bundles
A^{[2]}
and
C_{[2]}
by

\tilde{\mathrm{\Theta}}^{2}:A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}\to E^{2},\hskip 9.24994pt(a,c)\mapsto\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}(a)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c

(3.13)

(We recall that
F_{[k]}
stands for the graded bundle defined on the total space of the VB
F\rightarrow M
by assigning weight
k
to linear functions on
F
.)
The map
\tilde{\mathrm{\Theta}}^{2}
is a surjective morphism of graded bundles, hence
E^{2}
can be identified as the quotient of
A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}
by the equivalence relations
\sim
, where

(a,c)\sim(a^{\prime},c^{\prime})\iff\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}(a)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c=\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}(a^{\prime})\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}c^{\prime}.

Since
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
covers the identity
\operatorname{id}_{A}
, the elements
a
and
a^{\prime}\in A^{[2]}
project to the same element in
A
. As
E^{2}\rightarrow E^{1}
is an affine bundle, we can write
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}(a^{\prime})\bm{\boldsymbol{-}}\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}(a)=\widehat{\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}}(a^{\prime}-a)=c^{\prime}-c\in C
.
In other words,
(a^{\prime},c^{\prime})\bm{\boldsymbol{-}}(a,c)=(a^{\prime}-a,c^{\prime}-c)
is in the graph of the map
-\partial
, which is a subset of
A\times_{M}C
, the core of the graded bundle
A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}
. Therefore, what we need to define
E^{2}
is only the map
\partial
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
be a skew, order-two HA.

(i)

There is a canonical isomorphism of graded bundles

E^{2}\simeq{\raisebox{1.88887pt}{$(A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.88887pt}{$\operatorname{graph}(-\partial)$}\right.}

where
\partial:A\rightarrow C
is given in (
3.12
).

(ii)

A choice of local frames
(e_{i})
and
(c_{\mu})
of
\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
and
\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
, respectively, gives rise to a graded coordinate system
(x^{a},y^{i},w^{\mu})
for
E^{2}
(considered as the quotient of
A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}
), defined by
w^{\mu}=c_{\mu}^{\ast}+Q^{\mu}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}
. The composition
A^{[2]}\hookrightarrow A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}\rightarrow{\raisebox{1.88887pt}{$(A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.88887pt}{$\operatorname{graph}(-\partial)$}\right.}
coincides with the map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
which, in the introduced coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i},w^{\mu})
, read as

(\mathrm{\Theta}^{2})^{\ast}(x^{a})=x^{a},\hskip 9.24994pt(\mathrm{\Theta}^{2})^{\ast}(y^{i})=y^{i},\hskip 9.24994pt(\mathrm{\Theta}^{2})^{\ast}(w^{\mu})=Q^{\mu}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}.

Definition 3.7
.

We call
(x^{a},y^{i},w^{\mu})
an
adapted
system of graded coordinated on a HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
. It is uniquely defined once we set a system of local frames
(e_{i})
,
(c_{\mu})
, and is characterised by the equality
Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}=0
.

Proof.

Set
\tilde{E}^{2}=A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}
,
V=\operatorname{graph}(-\partial)
.
We have already shown that there is a well defined bijection between
E^{2}
and the quotient

{\raisebox{1.84995pt}{$\tilde{E}^{2}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.84995pt}{$\sim$}\right.}

defined as the set of equivalence classes of the following equivalence relation:
e\sim e^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
v\in V
such that
e^{\prime}=e\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}v
. It is also evident that this bijection is an isomorphism of graded bundles since it is a special case of the following more general construction: given a graded bundle
E^{k}
of order
k
and a vector subbundle
V\rightarrow M
of the core
\widehat{E^{k}}
, the quotient
{\raisebox{1.84995pt}{$E^{k}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.84995pt}{$V$}\right.}
which is the orbit space of the action on
E^{k}
of the subbundle
V\subset\widehat{E^{k}}
of the core,
inherits a graded bundle structure from
E^{k}
.

Recall that
(y_{i})
and
(c_{\mu}^{\ast})
denote the dual frames to
(e_{i})
and
(c_{\mu})
, respectively. Then
(x^{a},y^{i},\dot{y}^{i},c_{\mu}^{\ast})
forms a graded coordinate system on
A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}
. The introduced equivalence relation on this space reads as:
(x^{a},y^{i},\dot{y}^{i},c_{\mu}^{\ast})\sim(\underline{x}^{a},\underline{y}^{i},\dot{\underline{y}}^{i},\underline{c}_{\mu}^{\ast})
if and only if
x^{a}=\underline{x}^{a}
,
y^{i}=\underline{y}^{i}
,
\underline{c}_{\mu}^{\ast}-c_{\mu}^{\ast}=Q^{\mu}_{i}(\dot{y}^{i}-\dot{\underline{y}}^{i})
. Therefore, the functions
w^{\mu}:=c_{\mu}^{\ast}+Q^{\mu}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}
are well-defined on the quotient
{\raisebox{1.84995pt}{$\tilde{E}^{2}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.84995pt}{${\sim}$}\right.}
, and
(x^{a},y^{i},w^{\mu})
is a graded coordinate system on this quotient.

Let
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu})
be as in (
3.11
). The map
\tilde{\mathrm{\Theta}}^{2}:\tilde{E}^{2}\rightarrow E^{2}
, defined in (
3.13
), is given by

\left(\tilde{\mathrm{\Theta}}^{2}\right)^{\ast}(x^{a})=x^{a},\left(\tilde{\mathrm{\Theta}}^{2}\right)^{\ast}(y^{i})=y^{i},\left(\tilde{\mathrm{\Theta}}^{2}\right)^{\ast}(z^{\mu})=c_{\mu}^{\ast}+Q^{\mu}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}y^{i}y^{j}=w^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}y^{i}y^{j}.

Hence, the isomorphism from point (
i
), denoted by
I:{\raisebox{1.84995pt}{$\tilde{E}^{2}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.84995pt}{${\sim}$}\right.}\to E^{2}
, is given by
I^{\ast}(z^{\mu})=w^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}y^{i}y^{j}
, and the composition of the inclusion
A^{[2]}\hookrightarrow A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}
with
\tilde{\mathrm{\Theta}}^{2}
coincides with (
3.3
), i.e., with the formula for
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
, which proves the claim from point (
ii
) and completes the proof.
∎

3.2.2
The structure maps of a skew HA of order two

The subspace
\mathfrak{X}_{-2}(E^{2})\subset\mathfrak{X}(E^{2})
of vector fields of weight
-2
is a locally free
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-module canonically isomorphic to the space of sections of the core bundle
C=\widehat{E^{2}}
. We shall often identify these spaces without further comment. In coordinates as in (
3.11
), the isomorphism takes
c_{\mu}
to
{c_{\mu}^{\uparrow}}=\partial_{z^{\mu}}
, see Lemma
2.6
.

The reduction of
\kappa^{2}
to order one yields a skew algebroid, whose structure maps will be denoted by
[\cdot,\cdot]
and
\sharp:=\sharp^{1}:A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
. We assume that
\kappa^{2}
has a local form introduced in (
2.21
). Then

\sharp e_{i}=Q^{a}_{i}\partial_{x^{a}},\hskip 9.24994pt[e_{i},e_{j}]=Q^{k}_{ij}e_{k},

where
(e_{i})
is a local frame of sections of
A\to M
which is dual to the frame
(y^{i})
.
The core of the anchor map
\sharp^{2}:E^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}M
provides a VB morphism

\sharp^{C}:C\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M,\hskip 9.24994pt\sharp^{C}(c_{\mu})=Q^{a}_{\mu}\partial_{x^{a}}.

(3.14)

In more detail,
\sharp^{C}
is the composition of
\widehat{\sharp^{2}}:\widehat{E^{2}}\to\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}
with the isomorphism
\widehat{T^{2}M}\simeq\mathrm{T}M
, see (
2.9
).

The next mapping is a VB morphism
\tilde{\partial}:A\rightarrow C
defined by
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-linear map

s\mapsto{\frac{1}{2}{s}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-2}(E^{2})\simeq\operatorname{\Gamma}(C),

(3.15)

where
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
, see (
2.29
) for algebroid lifts. (It will turn out soon that
\tilde{\partial}=\partial
, the core of the map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
.)
In a similar manner we define

\beta:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C),\hskip 9.24994pt\beta(s_{1},s_{2})={\frac{1}{2}\,[{s_{1}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},{s_{2}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}]}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-2}(E^{2})\simeq\operatorname{\Gamma}(C),

(3.16)

which is a skew-symmetric mapping and

\Box:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C),\hskip 9.24994pt\Box_{s}v=[{s}^{\langle{0}\rangle},v]\in\mathfrak{X}_{-2}(E^{2})\simeq\Gamma(C)

(3.17)

called
the action of
A
on
C
.
The system of equations (
2.21
) describing
\kappa^{2}
results in the following formulas for the algebroid lifts
{e_{k}}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}
:

\begin{cases}{e_{k}}^{\langle{0}\rangle}&=Q^{a}_{k}\partial_{x^{a}}+Q_{jk}^{i}y^{j}\partial_{y^{i}}+(Q^{\mu}_{\nu k}\,z^{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\,Q_{ij,k}^{\mu}\,y^{i}y^{j})\,\partial_{z^{\mu}},\\
{e_{k}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}&=\partial_{y^{k}}+Q^{\mu}_{ik}\,y^{i}\,\partial_{z^{\mu}},\\
{e_{k}}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}&={2}Q^{\mu}_{k}\,\partial_{z^{\mu}}.\end{cases}

(3.18)

From this, we can easily derive the coordinate expressions for the introduced mappings
\tilde{\partial},\beta,\Box
:

\begin{cases}\tilde{\partial}(e_{i})&=Q^{\mu}_{i}c_{\mu},\\
\beta(e_{i},e_{j})&=Q^{\mu}_{[ij]}c_{\mu}\\
\Box_{e_{i}}c_{\nu}&=-Q^{\mu}_{\nu i}c_{\mu}.\end{cases}

(3.19)

where
Q^{\mu}_{[ij]}
is given in (
3.4
),
and the minus sign in the last line arises from our preference for working with left actions. Note that
\tilde{\partial}
coincides with
\partial
as
\widehat{\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}}(e_{i})=Q^{\mu}_{i}c_{\mu}
, see (
3.3
).

The symmetric part
Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}
of
Q^{\mu}_{ij}
is involved
12
12

12

The assignment
(e_{i},e_{j})\mapsto Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}c_{\mu}
does not give rise to a globally defined map. Change
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu})
to
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}u^{\mu}_{ij})
gives another assignment.

in the canonical map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}:A^{[2]}\rightarrow E^{2}
(see equations (
3.3
)).
It turns out that the remaining structure functions
Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}
alone do not define any geometric mapping. Instead, it is the functions

\tilde{Q}^{\mu}_{ijk}=Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}-Q^{l}_{jk}Q^{\mu}_{li}-Q^{l}_{ik}Q^{\mu}_{lj}+Q^{\nu}_{ji}Q^{\mu}_{\nu k}-Q^{a}_{k}\frac{\partial Q^{\mu}_{ji}}{\partial x^{a}}

(3.20)

that give a mapping

\delta:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C),\delta(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k})=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{Q}^{\mu}_{ijk}c_{\mu}.

(3.21)

A coordinate-free definition of
\delta
is

\delta(s_{1},s_{2},s)=\frac{1}{2}\,[{s_{1}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},[{s_{2}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},{s}^{\langle{0}\rangle}]]\in\mathfrak{X}_{-2}(E^{2})\simeq\operatorname{\Gamma}(C).

(3.22)

It is just a matter of direct computation of Lie brackets to show (
3.21
).
Introduce

{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2}):=\frac{1}{2}\delta(s_{1},s_{2},s)+\frac{1}{2}\delta(s_{2},s_{1},s)\,\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994pt{\delta}^{\mathrm{alt}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2}):=\frac{1}{2}\delta(s_{1},s_{2},s)-\frac{1}{2}\delta(s_{2},s_{1},s),

(3.23)

so
\delta={\delta}^{\mathrm{alt}}+{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}
. The skew-symmetric part
{\delta}^{\mathrm{alt}}_{s}
of
\delta(\cdot,\cdot,s)
satisfies

{\delta}^{\mathrm{alt}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2}):=\frac{1}{2}\delta(s_{1},s_{2},s)-\frac{1}{2}\delta(s_{2},s_{1},s)={\frac{1}{4}}[[{s_{1}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},{s_{2}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}],{s}^{\langle{0}\rangle}]={-\frac{1}{2}}\Box_{s}\beta(s_{1},s_{2}).

(3.24)

Further decomposition of
{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}
by means of the Schur decomposition
V\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{2}V=\operatorname{Sym}^{3}V\oplus W

(where
W
is the kernel of the total symmetrization map) yields no additional information as

\sum_{g\in\mathbb{S}_{3}}\delta(s_{g(1)},s_{g(2)},s_{g(3)})=0
due to the Jacobi identity for vector fields.
It will be convenient to work with

\omega:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C),\hskip 9.24994pt\omega(s_{1},s_{2},s)=\delta(s_{1},s_{2},s)-\beta(s_{1},[s_{2},s])

(3.25)

and its symmetric part

{{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2}):=\frac{1}{2}\omega(s_{1},s_{2},s)+\frac{1}{2}\omega(s_{2},s_{1},s)}

(3.26)

instead of
\delta
and
{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}
. In local coordinates we have
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{e_{k}}(e_{i},e_{j}):={\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\omega}_{ij,k}^{\mu}c_{\mu}
where

\bar{\omega}_{ij,k}^{\mu}=Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}+Q^{l}_{kj}Q^{\mu}_{(il)}+Q^{l}_{ki}Q^{\mu}_{(jl)}+Q^{a}_{j}\frac{\partial Q^{l}_{ik}}{\partial x^{a}}Q^{\mu}_{l}+Q^{a}_{i}\frac{\partial Q^{l}_{jk}}{\partial x^{a}}+Q^{a}_{k}\frac{Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}}{\partial x^{a}}.

(3.27)

Note that
{\omega}^{\mathrm{alt}}:=\omega-{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}
satisfies

{\omega}^{\mathrm{alt}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2})=-\frac{1}{2}\Box_{s}\beta(s_{1},s_{2})-\frac{1}{2}\beta(s_{1},[s_{2},s])+\frac{1}{2}\beta(s_{2},[s_{1},s]),

(3.28)

due to (
3.24
).

Example 3.8
.

We shall describe the structure maps of
(A^{[2]},\kappa^{[2]})
– the second order prolongation
A^{[2]}
of an AL algebroid
(A,\kappa)
.
In standard coordinates
(x^{a},y^{i},dx^{a},dy^{i})
on
\mathrm{T}A
it is given locally by the equations
dx^{a}=Q^{a}_{i}y^{i}
, hence

(x^{a},y^{i},dy^{i})
form a coordinate chart for
A^{[2]}
.
The coordinate description of
\kappa^{[2]}\subseteq\mathrm{T}^{2}A\times\mathrm{T}A^{[2]}
is

\kappa^{[2]}:\begin{cases}\dot{x}^{a}=&Q^{a}_{i}\,\underline{y}^{i}\\
\ddot{x}^{a}=&\frac{1}{2}\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}\,\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}+Q^{a}_{i}\,\underline{dy}^{i},\\
\dot{\underline{x}}^{a}=&Q^{a}_{i}\,y^{i}\\
\dot{\underline{y}}^{i}=&\dot{y}^{i}+Q^{i}_{jk}\,\underline{y}^{j}y^{k}\\
\left(\underline{dy}^{l}\right)^{\cdot}=&\ddot{y}^{l}+Q^{l}_{ij}\,\underline{dy}^{i}\,y^{j}+Q^{l}_{ij}\,\underline{y}^{i}\dot{y}^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{Q}^{l}_{ij,k}\underline{y}^{i}\underline{y}^{j}y^{k},\end{cases}

(3.29)

where
\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}
are defined in (
3.7
) and

\widehat{Q}^{l}_{ij,k}=\frac{\partial Q^{l}_{ik}}{\partial x^{a}}Q^{a}_{j}+\frac{\partial Q^{l}_{jk}}{\partial x^{a}}Q^{a}_{i}.

(3.30)

We find that
\partial:A\rightarrow\widehat{A^{[2]}}
defined in (
3.15
), coincides with the identity on
A
, with respect to the isomorphism given in Lemma
2.16
,

\partial=\operatorname{id}_{A}:A\rightarrow\widehat{A^{[2]}}\simeq A.

Moreover,
(A^{[2]},\kappa^{[2]})
is Lie, so
{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{[2]}}}
is a Lie algebra morphism. Hence,
\beta(s_{1},s_{2})=\frac{1}{2}[{s_{1}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},{s_{2}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}]=\frac{1}{2}{[s_{1},s_{2}]_{A}}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}
, see Theorem
2.11
. Thus we may write
\beta(s_{1},s_{2})=[s_{1},s_{2}]_{A}
up to the isomorphism
\widehat{A^{[2]}}\simeq A
. Similarly,
\Box_{s}v=[s,v]_{A}
,
\delta(s_{1},s_{2},s)=[[s_{1},s_{2}]_{A},s]_{A}
and
{\omega}^{\mathrm{alt}}=0
from (
3.28
) and the Jacobi identity. Moreover,
\sharp^{C}=\sharp
by Lemma
2.16
.

Remark 3.9
.

We can analogously define the following structure maps for any order
k
HA
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
:

\phi_{{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,{\alpha}_{n}}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=\frac{1}{k!}[\ldots[[{s_{1}}^{\langle{-{\alpha}_{1}}\rangle},{s_{2}}^{\langle{-{\alpha}_{2}}\rangle}],{s_{3}}^{\langle{-{\alpha}_{3}}\rangle}],\ldots,{s_{n}}^{\langle{-{\alpha}_{n}}\rangle}]\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}}),

where
s_{i}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
and
0\leq{\alpha}_{i}
are such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\alpha}_{i}=k
. In particular,
s\mapsto\frac{1}{k!}{s}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}
defines a VB morphism

\partial^{k}:A\to\widehat{E^{k}},\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}})\simeq\mathfrak{X}_{-k}(E^{k}).

Moreover, we have the structure maps

\Box:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}})\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\Box_{s}v=[{s}^{[0]},v]\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{E^{k}})\\

and

\sharp^{E^{k}}:\widehat{E^{k}}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M

(3.31)

defined as the core of the anchor map
\sharp^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}M
composed with the isomorphism
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{k}M}\simeq\mathrm{T}M
given in (
2.7
).
If
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
is Lie then, due to Theorem
2.11
,

\phi_{{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,{\alpha}_{n}}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=\frac{1}{k!}{\left([\ldots[[s_{1},s_{2}]_{A},s_{3}]_{A},\ldots,s_{n}]_{A}\right)}^{\langle{-k}\rangle}
, so all the structure maps
\phi_{{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,{\alpha}_{n}}
with fixed
n
coincide with
\phi_{k,0,\ldots,0}
.

Let us assume that
(A,\sharp,[\cdot,\cdot]_{A})
is a Lie algebroid. Then
(A^{[k]},\kappa^{[k]})
is a Lie HA. The map
\phi_{k}:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(\widehat{A^{[k]}})
gives the identification
A\simeq\widehat{A^{[k]}}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
which coincides with
\jmath^{[k-1]}_{A}:A\rightarrow\widehat{A^{[k]}}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k-1}A
,
and

\phi_{{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,{\alpha}_{n}}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=[\ldots[[s_{1},s_{2}]_{A},s_{3}]_{A},\ldots,s_{n}]_{A},

while
\Box_{s}v=[s,v]_{A}
.

We introduce a few additional maps, denoted by
\xi
,
\psi
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
,
associated with a skew HA of order two. It will turn out that if
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
is AL then all these maps, except for
\varepsilon_{1}
, vanish. If
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
is Lie then also
\varepsilon_{1}
is zero. These maps will be used in formulation of tensor-like properties of the structure maps we have already introduced.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
be a skew HA,
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
. We define

\displaystyle\xi:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}(M),\hskip 9.24994pt\xi(s_{1},s_{2})=\sharp[s_{1},s_{2}]-[\sharp s_{1},\sharp s_{2}],

(3.32)

\displaystyle\psi:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}(M),\hskip 9.24994pt\psi(s_{1},s_{2})(f):={\frac{1}{2}}{s_{1}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}{s_{2}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}((\sharp^{2})^{\ast}\ddot{f})-(\sharp s_{1})(\sharp s_{2})(f),

(3.33)

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\sharp^{C}\circ\partial-\sharp:A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M,

(3.34)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}(s_{1},s_{2})=[{s_{1}}^{\langle{-k}\rangle},{s_{2}}^{\langle{-2+k}\rangle}]-{[s_{1},s_{2}]}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-2}(E^{2})\simeq\operatorname{\Gamma}(C),

(3.35)

where
k=0
or
1
.

Lemma 3.11
.

The maps
\xi
,
\psi
,
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
introduced in Definition
3.10
have the following properties:

(i)

The maps
\xi
and
\varepsilon_{1}
are tensorial in both arguments, so they give rise to the VB morphisms
\xi:\bigwedge^{2}A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M
and
\varepsilon_{1}:\bigwedge^{2}A\rightarrow C
, respectively. Moreover,
\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{1}(s_{1},s_{2})=\beta(s_{1},s_{2})-\partial([s_{1},s_{2}])
.

(ii)

The map
(s_{1},s_{2})\mapsto\varepsilon_{0}(s_{1},s_{2})
is tensorial in
s_{2}
, bot not in
s_{1}
, in general. We have
\varepsilon_{0}(fs_{1},s_{2})-f\varepsilon_{0}(s_{1},s_{2})=\varepsilon(s_{2})(f)\partial(s_{1})
. Moreover,
{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon_{0}(s_{1},s_{2})=\Box_{s_{1}}(\partial s_{2})-\partial([s_{1},s_{2}])
.

(iii)

\psi(s_{1},s_{2})
is a derivation, hence the codomain of
\psi
is correctly defined. Moreover,
\psi(s_{1},s_{2})
is tensorial in
s_{1}
, but it is not tensorial in
s_{2}
, in general. Namely,

\psi(s_{1},gs_{2})=g\psi(s_{1},s_{2})+(\sharp s_{1})(g)\cdot{\varepsilon(s_{2})},

In coordinates,

\psi(e_{k^{\prime}},e_{k})={\frac{1}{2}}\left(Q^{\mu}_{k^{\prime}k}Q^{a}_{\mu}+Q^{a}_{k^{\prime}k}-2Q^{b}_{k^{\prime}}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}}\right)\partial_{x^{a}}.

(3.36)

The skew-symmetric part of
\psi
,
{\psi}^{\mathrm{alt}}(s_{1},s_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\psi(s_{1},s_{2})-\frac{1}{2}\psi(s_{2},s_{1})
, is expressed in terms of the other structure maps:

{\psi}^{\mathrm{alt}}(s_{1},s_{2})={\frac{1}{2}}\sharp^{C}(\beta(s_{1},s_{2}))-{\frac{1}{2}}[\sharp s_{1},\sharp s_{2}].

(3.37)

The symmetric part of
\psi
,
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}=\psi-{\psi}^{\mathrm{alt}}
, writes in coordinates as
13
13

13

It is tempting to consider a mapping
(e_{i},e_{j})\mapsto\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}\partial_{x^{a}}
. However, one can easily check that it does not give rise to a globally defined map.

{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}(e_{i},e_{j})={\frac{1}{2}}\left(Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}+Q^{a}_{ij}-\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}\right)\partial_{x^{a}},

(3.38)

where
\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}
are defined in (
3.7
).
Moreover, the condition
\sharp^{2}\circ\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}=\sharp^{[2]}
(compare with Theorem
3.2
) is equivalent to the conjunction
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}=0
and
\sharp^{C}\circ\partial=\sharp
.

The proof is given in Appendix, subsection
4.2
.

It turns out that the map
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}
corresponds to a certain graded bundle morphism. A slightly more general result holds:

Lemma 3.12
.

(a) Let
(A\rightarrow M,\sharp:A\to\mathrm{T}M)
be an anchored vector bundle, and let
\rho:A\to C
be a VB morphism . Then, symmetric maps

\Psi:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
satisfying

\Psi(s_{1},fs_{2})=f\Psi(s_{1},s_{2})+(\sharp s_{1})(f)\rho(s_{2})

(3.39)

are in a one-to-one correspondence with graded bundle morphisms
\Phi:A^{[2]}\rightarrow C_{[2]}
.
The corresponding graded bundle morphism
\Phi:A^{[2]}\to C_{[2]}
has the local form

\Phi(x^{a},y^{i},\dot{y}^{i})=\left(\rho^{\mu}_{i}(x)\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{\mu}_{ij}(x)y^{i}y^{j}\right)c_{\mu},

(3.40)

where
\rho(e_{i})=\rho^{\mu}_{i}c_{\mu}
and
\Psi(e_{i},e_{j})=\Psi^{\mu}_{ij}c_{\mu}
, and
(e_{i})
(respectively,
(c_{\mu})
) is a local frame of sections of the vector bundle
A
(respectively,
C
).

The proof is given in Appendix, subsection
4.2
.

The structure maps defined above,
\beta
,
\Box
,
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}
, and
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}
are not
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-linear in general, but satisfy certain tensor-like identities presented in the following result.

Theorem 3.13

(order-two skew HAs)
.

(a) Let
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
be a skew higher algebroid of order two,
A=E^{1}
,
C=\widehat{E^{2}}
. Let
v\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
,
s,s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
. Then

•

The map
\beta
is skew-symmetric and

\beta(s_{1},f\,s_{2})=f\,\beta(s_{1},s_{2})+(\sharp s_{1})(f)\partial(s_{2}).

•

The map
(s,v)\mapsto\Box_{s}v
satisfies

\displaystyle\Box_{f\,s}v

\displaystyle=f\Box_{s}v-(\sharp^{C}v)(f)\,\partial(s),

\displaystyle\Box_{s}(fv)

\displaystyle=f\Box_{s}v+(\sharp s)(f)v.

•

The symmetric map
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}
satisfies

{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}(s_{1},f\,s_{2})=f\,{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}(s_{1},s_{2})+\frac{1}{2}(\sharp s_{1})(f)\varepsilon(s_{2}).

•

The map
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2})
is symmetric in
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
and satisfies

{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{s}(s_{1},fs_{2})=f{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2})-\frac{1}{2}(\sharp s_{1})(f)\cdot{\varepsilon_{0}(s,s_{2})}+{\frac{1}{2}}\xi(s,s_{1})(f)\partial(s_{2}),

{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{fs}(s_{1},s_{2})=f{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2})+\frac{1}{4}\left(\sharp s_{1}(f)\varepsilon_{1}(s_{2},s)+\sharp s_{2}(f)\varepsilon_{1}(s_{1},s)\right)+{{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}(s_{1},s_{2})(f)\partial(s)},

(The maps
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\xi
, and
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}
are as in Definition
3.10
.)

(b) Conversely,
let
(A,[\cdot,\cdot],\sharp)
be a skew algebroid and
C\rightarrow M
be a vector bundle.
Then a system of the following maps:

(i)

VB morphisms
\partial:A\rightarrow C
and
\sharp^{C}:C\to\mathrm{T}M
covering the identity on
M
,

(ii)

a skew-symmetric map
\beta:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
satisfying (
•
‣
3.13
),

(iii)

a map
\Box:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)\to\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
satisfying (
•
‣
3.13
) and (
•
‣
3.13
),

(iv)

a symmetric map
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
satisfying (
•
‣
3.13
),

(v)

a map
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\to\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
satisfying
(
•
‣
3.13
) and (
•
‣
3.13
),

define a skew order-two HA on the graded bundle
E^{2}={\raisebox{1.88887pt}{$(A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.88887pt}{$(\operatorname{graph}(-\partial))$}\right.}
(see Lemma
3.6
) uniquely.
(Note that the maps
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\xi
, which appear in the Leibniz-type identities of the structure maps listed here, can be expressed in terms of the aforementioned maps, see Definition
3.10
and Lemma
3.11
.)

Proof.

The proof of part (a) – regarding the tensor-like properties of the structure maps
\beta
,
\sharp^{C}
,
\Box
,
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}
, and
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}
listed above – is technical and has been moved to Appendix, Subsection
4.2
.

Proof of part (b): Let
(A,[\cdot,\cdot],\sharp)
be a skew algebroid, and assume the structure maps listed above,
\partial
,
\beta
,
\Box
,
{\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}
, and
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}
, are given.

Given the VB morphisms
\partial:A\to C
and
\sharp:A\to\mathrm{T}M
, the construction of the graded bundle
E^{2}
as the quotient of
A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}
is well-founded, see Lemma
3.6
. We shall now present the construction of the graded bundle morphism
\sharp^{2}:E^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}M
.

There is a graded bundle morphism
\Phi:A^{[2]}\rightarrow(\mathrm{T}M)_{[2]}
corresponding to
\Psi={\psi}^{\mathrm{sym}}
and
\rho=\varepsilon
, as explained in Lemma
3.12
. Define a map

\tilde{\sharp}^{2}:A^{[2]}\times_{M}C_{[2]}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}M,\hskip 9.24994pt(a^{2},v)\mapsto\sharp^{[2]}(a^{2})\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}(\sharp^{C}v+\Phi(a^{2})),

(3.41)

where
a^{2}\in A^{[2]}
and
v\in C
project to the same point in
M
, and
\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}
denotes the action of the core bundle
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}\simeq\mathrm{T}M
on
\mathrm{T}^{2}M
. We shall show that this map factors through the action of the graph of
-\partial
, the subbundle of the core bundle
A\times_{M}C
, giving rise to a map from the quotient graded bundle
E^{2}
, constructed in Lemma
3.6
. It remains to show that

\sharp^{[2]}(a^{2}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}b)\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}(\sharp^{C}(v-\partial b)+\Phi(a^{2}\bm{\boldsymbol{+}}b))

does not depend on
b\in A
. Indeed, the change in the core is equal to

\widehat{\sharp^{[2]}}(b)-(\sharp^{C}\circ\partial)(b)+\widehat{\Phi}(b)=\sharp b-\sharp^{C}\circ\partial b+\varepsilon(b)=0,

since
\widehat{\sharp^{[2]}}=\sharp
and by the definition of
\varepsilon
. A direct calculation from the coordinate formulas (
3.38
) and (
3.40
) shows that the resulting map
E^{2}\to\mathrm{T}^{2}M
is indeed given by the desired formula:

\begin{split}{(\sharp^{2})}^{*}(\ddot{x}^{a})={(\sharp^{[2]})}^{*}(\ddot{x}^{2})+{(\sharp^{C})}^{*}(\dot{x}^{a})+{\Phi}^{*}(\dot{x}^{a})=\\
Q^{a}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}y^{i}y^{j}+Q^{a}_{\mu}c_{\mu}^{\ast}+(Q^{\mu}_{i}Q^{a}_{\mu}-Q^{a}_{i})\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}(Q^{a}_{ij}-\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij})y^{i}y^{j}=Q^{a}_{\mu}w^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{a}_{ij}y^{i}y^{j},\end{split}

where
(x^{a},y^{i},w^{\mu})
is the adapted coordinate system on the quotient, so
c_{\mu}^{\ast}=w^{\mu}-Q^{\mu}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}
and
Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}=0
, see Definition
3.7
.

We show now how to recover the comorphism
\kappa^{2}
, which covers the graded bundle
\sharp^{2}
and governs the HA structure on the graded bundle
E^{2}
.
All the local structure functions
(Q^{\cdots}_{\cdots})=Q^{a}_{i}
,
Q^{a}_{ij}
,
Q^{a}_{\mu}
,
Q^{i}_{jk}
,
Q^{\mu}_{i}
,
Q^{\mu}_{ij}
,
Q^{\mu}_{\nu i}
,
Q^{\mu}_{ij,k})
can be derived from the structure maps listed above once we fix a graded coordinate system
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu})
on
E^{2}
. (All these functions are defined locally, over an open subset
U\subset M
.) Without loss of generality, we may assume that
(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu})
is an adapted coordinate system (Definition
3.7
), so
Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}=0
.

The local structure functions
Q^{a}_{i}
,
Q^{a}_{\mu}
, and
Q^{a}_{ij}
are derived from the map
\sharp^{2}
. Next,
Q^{\mu}_{[ij]}=Q^{\mu}_{ij}
and
Q^{\mu}_{\nu i}
are derived from the maps
\beta
and
\Box
, respectively, by means of (
3.19
). Finally,
Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}
is determined from
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}
, see (
3.27
).

The structure functions
(Q^{\cdots}_{\cdots})
establish a HA structure
(E^{2}_{U},\kappa^{2}_{U})
over the base
U
, through the equations (
2.21
), where
E^{2}_{U}=(\sigma^{2})^{-1}(U)
. Moreover, the comorphism
\kappa^{2}_{U}
determines all the structure maps
\partial_{U}
,
\sharp^{C}_{U}
,
\beta_{U}
,
\Box_{U}
,
\psi_{U}
, and
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{U}
which are defined on sections of the vector bundles
\sigma^{1}_{U}:A_{U}\rightarrow U
and
\widehat{\sigma^{2}_{U}}:C_{U}\rightarrow U
.
Also the other structure maps present in the formulation of our theorem, the maps
\varepsilon_{U}
,
(\varepsilon_{0})_{U}
,
(\varepsilon_{1})_{U}
and
\xi
are determined by
\kappa^{2}_{U}
, as explained in Definition
3.10
.

These maps are consistent with the restrictions of the corresponding maps given at the outset as the latter are local operators and satisfy the same Leibniz-type identities.
For example,
\Box_{U}(e_{i},c_{\mu})=\Box|_{U}(e_{i},c_{\mu})
by (
3.19
), where on the RHS,
\Box|_{U}
denotes the given structure map
\Box
restricted to
\operatorname{\Gamma}_{U}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}_{U}(C)
. Moreover, both
\Box_{U}
and
\Box|_{U}
satisfy the same Leibniz-type identities given in (
•
‣
3.13
) and (
•
‣
3.13
), because
\partial_{U}
coincides with
\partial|_{\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)}
, and similarly for
\sharp
and
\sharp^{C}
. Therefore,
(\Box_{U})(s|_{U},v|_{U})=\Box_{s}v
, for any
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
and
v\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(C)
.

Therefore, if
U\cap U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, then
(\kappa^{2}_{U})\Big|_{U^{\prime}\cap U}
and
(\kappa^{2}_{U^{\prime}})\Big|_{U\cap U^{\prime}}
coincide with
\kappa^{2}_{U\cap U^{\prime}}
, which is defined by the restrictions of the structure maps to the sections over
U\cap U^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\kappa^{2}
is globally well-defined.

∎

Remark 3.14
.

The map
\beta
in the formulation of part (b) of Theorem
3.13
can be replaced by the VB morphism
\varepsilon_{1}:\bigwedge^{2}A\rightarrow C
, defined in (
3.35
). Indeed, the map
\beta
is related to
\varepsilon_{1}
via the formula given in Lemma
3.11
,
\beta(s_{1},s_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{1}(s_{1},s_{2})+\partial([s_{1},s_{2}])
. Hence, the Leibniz-type identity (
•
‣
3.13
) follows from the Leibinz rule of the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
on
\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
. Note also that the anchor
\sharp:A\to\mathrm{T}M
is uniquely determined by the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
on
\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
.

3.2.3
HAs over a point

We shall study HAs
(\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k})
in which the base
M=\{\operatorname{pt}\}
is a point. Any such structure is fully described by a weight-respecting mapping (see Theorem
2.11
)

{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}:\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}(E^{k})

(3.42)

where the algebra
(\mathfrak{g},[\cdot,\cdot])
is defined as the order-one reduction of
(E^{k},\kappa^{k})
. Let
(e_{i})
be a basis of the vector space
E^{1}=\mathfrak{g}
,
(y^{i})
be the corresponding dual basis and let
(y^{i},z^{\mu})
be a graded coordinate system for
E^{k}
in which the weight
\mathrm{w}(y^{i})=1
and
\mathrm{w}(z^{\mu})\geq 2
. To define a HA
(\sigma^{k}:E^{k}\rightarrow M,\kappa^{k})

it amounts to provide vector fields
{e_{m}}^{\langle{-{\alpha}}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-{\alpha}}(E^{k})
for
0\leq{\alpha}\leq k
such that

\displaystyle{e_{m}}^{\langle{0}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}Q^{i}_{lm}y^{l}\partial_{y^{i}}+\sum_{\mu}f^{\mu}_{m}(y,z)\partial_{z^{\mu}},

(3.43)

\displaystyle{e_{m}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\partial_{y^{m}}+\sum_{\mu}g^{\mu}_{m}(y,z)\partial_{z^{\mu}},

(3.44)

where
Q^{i}_{lm}
are the structure constants for
(\mathfrak{g},[\cdot,\cdot])
, i.e.,
[e_{l},e_{m}]=Q^{i}_{lm}e_{i}
, see Theorem
2.11
. It follows that
f^{\mu}_{m}
(resp.
g^{\mu}_{m}
) are homogeneous functions on
E^{k}
of weight
\mathrm{w}(z^{\mu})
(resp.,
\mathrm{w}(z^{\mu})-1
).
The obtained (general) HA is AL if and only if the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
is skew-symmetric.

Order two.

The map
\mathrm{\Theta}^{k}:\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow E^{k}
covers
\mathrm{\Theta}^{1}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, hence it gives a canonical section of the bundle projection
\sigma^{k}_{1}:E^{k}\rightarrow E^{1}=\mathfrak{g}
. In case
k=2
,
\sigma^{2}_{1}:E^{2}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
is an affine bundle projection, hence the mapping
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}:\mathrm{T}\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{[1]}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{[2]}\rightarrow E^{2}
yields a canonical splitting

E^{2}=\mathfrak{g}_{[1]}\times C_{[2]},

where
C=\widehat{E^{2}}
, and
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}(x,0)=(x,0)
where
x\in\mathfrak{g}
.
We are going to describe the structure of the graded, finite dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}:=\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}(E^{2})
.
In standard graded coordinates
(y^{i},z^{\mu})
on
\mathfrak{g}\times C
, vector fields of non-positive weight
{\alpha}
, where
-2\leq{\alpha}\leq 0
, have the following form

X_{0}=c_{j}^{i}y^{j}\partial_{y^{i}}+(c^{\mu}_{\nu}\,z^{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\,c_{ij}^{\mu}\,y^{i}y^{j})\,\partial_{z^{\mu}},\hskip 9.24994ptX_{-1}=c^{i}\partial_{y^{i}}+c_{i}^{\mu}\,y^{i}\,\partial_{z^{\mu}},\hskip 9.24994ptX_{-2}=c^{\mu}\,\partial_{z^{\mu}}

where
c^{\cdots}_{\cdots}
are some constants, and
X_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\alpha}(E^{2})
. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}
acts faithfully on the linear subspace
\mathcal{A}_{\leq 2}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}\times C)
, spanned by homogenous functions of weight
\leq 2
. It has a
\mathbb{R}
-basis consisting of the functions
1,y^{i},y^{i}y^{j},z^{\mu}
and we have
\mathcal{A}_{\leq 2}\simeq\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}\oplus C^{\ast}\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
. By examining the action of the vector fields
X_{0}
,
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
, we easily find the following decomposition (compare with a more general Lemma
4.1
),

\mathfrak{X}_{0}\simeq\operatorname{End}(\mathfrak{g})\oplus\operatorname{End}(C)\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathfrak{g},C),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathfrak{X}_{-1}\simeq\mathfrak{g}\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{g},C),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathfrak{X}_{-2}\simeq C.

(3.45)

The formula for the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}
will be given in the proof of Theorem
3.15
, see (
4.9
).

We shall describe algebroid lifts
{e}^{\langle{-{\alpha}}\rangle}
, where
{\alpha}\in\{0,1,2\}
, by means of the structure maps of
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
. Then it will be straightforward to verify the condition given in Remark
2.12
, ensuring that
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
is a Lie HA.

Theorem 3.15
.

The structure of a skew, order-two HA over a point is fully determined by the linear maps
[\cdot,\cdot]:\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
,
\partial:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow C
,
\beta:\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow C
,
\Box:{\mathfrak{g}\otimes C}\rightarrow C
,
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}:\mathfrak{g}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow C
. The associated algebroid lifts
e\mapsto{e}^{\langle{-{\alpha}}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{-\alpha}
are given (with respect to the isomorphisms listed in (
3.45
)) by

\displaystyle{e}^{\langle{0}\rangle}

\displaystyle=[\cdot,e]\oplus{\Box_{-e}(\cdot)}\oplus{2{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{e}(\cdot,\cdot),}

\displaystyle{e}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}

\displaystyle=e\oplus\beta(\cdot,e),

\displaystyle{e}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\partial(e)

A skew HA
(\mathfrak{g}\times C,[\cdot,\cdot],\partial,\beta,\Box,{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}})
is Lie if and only if
\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra,
\Box
equips
C
with a
\mathfrak{g}
-module structure,
\partial:{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow C
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-module morphism,
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}=0
and the mapping
\beta
is given by

\beta(x_{1},x_{2})=\partial([x_{1},x_{2}]).

Hence, order-two Lie higher algebroids over a point are in a one-to-one correspondence with
\mathfrak{g}
-module morphisms
\partial:{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow{C}
.

The proof is straightforward but somewhat lengthy, so it has been moved to Appendix, Subsection
4.2
.

Order greater than two.

The graded bundle hosting a higher Lie algebroid over a point of order greater than two need not split in a canonical way. A simple example is provided by a non-split graded space
E^{k}
such that
E^{1}=\{0\}
– a vector space of dimension 0. In this case, the VB comorphism
{\overrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}}
must be the zero map, i.e.,
(\kappa^{k})_{a}:\mathrm{T}^{k}\{0\}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}_{a}E^{k}
is the zero map for any
a\in E^{k}
, as the domain is zero-dimensional. A concrete example of this is
E^{4}=\mathrm{T}_{q}^{2}M
, where
q\in M
is a fixed point on a manifold
M
, and the linear coordinates

(\dot{x}^{a})
on
\mathrm{T}_{q}M
are assigned weight
2
, while the weight of
\ddot{x}^{a}
is
4
.

Following the example given in
[
JR18
, Section 6]
, a graded Lie algebra
\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{i}
, where
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{g}
, equipped with a graded Lie algebra morphism
A:\mathrm{T}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathfrak{g}_{i}
such that
A^{0}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}
, gives rise to a split Lie higher algebroid of order
k
.

3.2.4
AL HAs of order two

There are a few relations among the structure maps of a skew HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
that we introduced above, ensuring that it is an almost Lie algebroid.

Theorem 3.16

(order-two AL HAs)
.

Let
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
be a skew order-two HA. Then
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
is AL if and only if

\displaystyle\text{$A$ is an almost Lie algebroid, i.e., $\xi=0$},

(
\mathrm{AL_{A}}
)

\displaystyle\sharp=\sharp^{C}\circ\partial,\text{i.e., }\varepsilon=0,

(
\mathrm{AL_{\partial}}
)

\displaystyle\psi=0,

(
\mathrm{AL_{\psi}}
)

\displaystyle\sharp^{C}(\Box_{s}v)=[\sharp s,\sharp^{C}v],

(
\mathrm{AL_{\Box}}
)

\displaystyle\sharp^{C}\circ\omega=0.

(
\mathrm{AL_{\omega}}
)

Corollary 3.17
.

An order-two AL HA on a graded bundle
E^{2}\rightarrow M
is defined by:

•

an AL algebroid structure on the vector bundle
A\rightarrow M
;

•

VB morphisms
\partial
,
\sharp^{C}
, and
\varepsilon_{1}
,

•

maps
\Box
and
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}
that satisfy the aforementioned Leibniz-type identities,

such that conditions (

AL
□

) and (

AL
∂
