Proof of Theorem
3.15
.

First, we shall describe the structure of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{X}_{\leq 0}(\mathfrak{g}\times C)
with respect to the decomposition given in (
3.45
).
We write
\phi\oplus\psi\oplus\chi\in\mathfrak{X}_{0}
,
x\oplus f\in\mathfrak{X}_{-1}
,
v\in\mathfrak{X}_{-2}
, where
\phi\in\operatorname{End}(\mathfrak{g})
,
\psi\in\operatorname{End}(C)
,
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathfrak{g},C)
,
x\in\mathfrak{g}
,
f\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathfrak{g},C)
, and
v\in C
. We have

\begin{split}[\mathfrak{X}_{0},\mathfrak{X}_{0}]:&[\phi_{1},\phi_{2}]=\phi_{2}\circ\phi_{1}-\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2},[\psi_{1},\psi_{2}]=\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}-\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2},[\chi_{1},\chi_{2}]=0\\
&[\phi,\chi](x_{1},x_{2})=\chi(\phi(x_{1}),x_{2})+\chi(x_{1},\phi(x_{2})),[\psi,\chi](x_{1},x_{2})=-\psi(\chi(x_{1},x_{2})),[\phi,\psi]=0,\\
[\mathfrak{X}_{0},\mathfrak{X}_{-1}]:&[\phi,x]=-\phi(x),[\phi,f]=f\circ\phi,[\psi,x]=0,[\psi,f]=-\psi\circ f,[\chi,x]=-\chi(x,\cdot),[\chi,f]=0\\
[\mathfrak{X}_{-1},\mathfrak{X}_{-1}]:&[x_{1},x_{2}]=0,[f_{1},f_{2}]=0,[f,x]=-f(x),\\
[\mathfrak{X}_{0},\mathfrak{X}_{-2}]:&[\phi,v]=0,[\psi,v]=-\psi(v),[\chi,v]=0.\end{split}

(4.9)

For example, the formula for
[\phi,x]
can be derived as follows. A vector
x=(x^{i})\in\mathfrak{g}
is idenified with the vector field
x^{i}\partial_{y^{i}}
, and an endomorphism
\phi:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
, such that
{\phi}^{*}(y^{i})=\phi^{i}_{j}y^{j}
, is identified with the vector field
\phi=\phi^{i}_{j}y^{j}\partial_{y^{i}}
. Then,
[\phi,x]=-\phi^{i}_{j}x^{j}\partial_{y^{i}}=-\phi(x)
. In a similar way we derive the remaining formulas.

The formulas for algebroid lifts
{e}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}
, where
{\alpha}=-2,-1,0
, given in the formulation of our theorem, define vector fields which have the form as in (
3.43
) since the projection of
{e}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}
onto
\mathfrak{g}
is
e
and the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
is skew-symmetric. Therefore, these vector fields define an AL higher algebroid.

Conversely, let
(\mathfrak{g}\times C,\kappa^{2})
be a skew HA defined by means of algebroid lifts
{e}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}
given above. Let us temporarily denote by
\widetilde{\partial}
,
\widetilde{\beta}
,
\widetilde{\Box}
,
\widetilde{{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}}
the maps associated with
\kappa^{2}
, defined in Subsection
3.2
by formulas (
3.15
), (
3.16
), (
3.17
), (
3.26
), respectively. We shall show that
\widetilde{\partial}=\partial
,
\widetilde{\beta}=\beta
,
\widetilde{\Box}=\Box
,
\widetilde{{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}}={\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}
.

The definitions of
\widetilde{\partial}
and
\partial
coincide, hence
\widetilde{\partial}=\partial
. For the proof of the equality
\widetilde{\beta}=\beta
we have

\widetilde{\beta}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}[{x}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},{y}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}]=\frac{1}{2}[x\oplus\beta(\cdot,x),y\oplus\beta(\cdot,y)]=\beta(x,y)

due to the skew-symmetry of
\beta
and the formulas (
4.9
) for
the bracket on
\mathfrak{X}_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{X}_{-1}
. For
\widetilde{\Box}=\Box
we write

{\widetilde{\Box}_{x}v=[{x}^{\langle{0}\rangle},v]=[\underbrace{[\cdot,x]}_{\phi}\oplus\underbrace{\Box_{-x}(\cdot)}_{\psi}\oplus\underbrace{2{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{x}(\cdot,\cdot)}_{\chi},v]=-\psi(v)=\Box_{x}v},

due to the formulas for the bracket restricted to
\mathfrak{X}_{-2}\oplus\mathfrak{X}_{0}
. The proof of
\widetilde{\delta}=\delta
is a bit longer. First, we calculate

\begin{split}[{y}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},{z}^{\langle{0}\rangle}]&=[y\oplus\beta(\cdot,y),[\cdot,z]\oplus\Box_{-z}(\cdot)\oplus 2{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{z}(\cdot,\cdot)]=\\
&\underbrace{[y,z]}_{X}\oplus\underbrace{\beta([\cdot,-z],y)+{\Box_{-z}\beta(\cdot,y)}+{2{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{z}(y,\cdot)}}_{F},\end{split}

(4.10)

hence

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}(x,y,z)

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{2}}[{x}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},[{y}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},{z}^{\langle{0}\rangle}]]=\frac{1}{2}[x\oplus\beta(\cdot,x),X\oplus F]=\frac{1}{2}F(x)-\frac{1}{2}\beta(X,x)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\beta([y,z],x)+\frac{1}{2}\beta([z,x],y)-\frac{1}{2}\Box_{z}{\beta(x,y)}+{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{z}(y,x).

Thus
\widetilde{{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{x}}(y,z)
, which is obtained by symmetrizing
\widetilde{\delta}(x,y,z)-\beta(x,[y,z])
in
x,y
, coincides, due to the skew-symmetry of
\beta
, with the symmetrization of
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{z}(x,y)+\frac{1}{2}\beta([y,z],x)+\frac{1}{2}\beta([z,x],y)
, and the latter simplifies to
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{z}(x,y)
, as was claimed.

We now examine the Lie condition for HAs given in Remark
2.12
.
From (
4.9
) we get

[{x}^{\langle{0}\rangle},{y}^{\langle{0}\rangle}]=[\underbrace{[-x,\cdot]}_{\phi_{1}}\oplus\underbrace{\Box_{-x}(\cdot)}_{\psi_{1}}\oplus\underbrace{2{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{x}(\cdot,\cdot)}_{\chi_{1}},\underbrace{[-y,\cdot]}_{\phi_{2}}\oplus\underbrace{\Box_{-y}(\cdot)}_{\psi_{2}}\oplus\underbrace{2{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{y}(\cdot,\cdot)}_{\chi_{2}}]=\\
\left([y,[x,\cdot]]-[x,[y,\cdot]]\right)\oplus[\Box_{y},\Box_{x}]\oplus\chi,

for some
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathfrak{g},C)
. From the condition
[{x}^{\langle{0}\rangle},{y}^{\langle{0}\rangle}]={[x,y]}^{\langle{0}\rangle}
we read that

\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra,
C
is a left
\mathfrak{g}
-module. We shall show that
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}=0
, from which it follows that the identity obtain by comparing the
\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathfrak{g},C)
-components is satisfied automatically.

The equation
[{x}^{\langle{-1}\rangle},{y}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}]={[x,y]}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}
and
[{x}^{\langle{0}\rangle},{y}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}]={[x,y]}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}
write as

\Box_{x}\partial(y)=\partial([x,y])\text{ and }\beta(x,y)=\partial([x,y]).

(4.11)

Finally, for
(i,j)=(0,-1)
, the Lie condition from (
4.10
) is given by

\beta(\cdot,[x,y])=\beta([\cdot,-x],y)+\Box_{-x}\beta(\cdot,y)+{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{x}(y,\cdot).

This, along with the equalities in (
4.11
) and the Jacobi identity, yields
{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}=0
, and completes the proof.
∎

4.3
Equations for AL and Lie HAs

We shall use Theorem
2.11
to write equations for structure functions corresponding to almost Lie HAs.
The obtained equations will be used to complete the proof of Theorem
3.16
.

AL HAs.

Let
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
be an AL HA and let
(e_{k})
,
(c_{\mu})
be as in Subsection
3.2
. The vector fields

{e_{k}}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\alpha}(E^{2})
for
{\alpha}=0,-1,-2
are given in (
3.18
).
The formulas for
(\mathrm{T}^{2}M,\kappa^{2}_{M})
-algebroid lifts
{\underline{e}_{k}}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}:={(\sharp e_{k})}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}={(Q^{a}_{k}\partial_{x^{a}})}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\alpha}(\mathrm{T}^{2}M)
are easily derived from (
2.31
) by notting that
{\partial_{x^{a}}}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}
is equal to
\partial_{x^{a}}
,
\partial_{\dot{x}^{a}}
and
2\partial_{\ddot{x}^{a}}
for
{\alpha}=0,-1,-2
, respectively. Thus

\begin{cases}{\underline{e}_{k}}^{\langle{0}\rangle}&=Q^{a}_{k}\partial_{x^{a}}+\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial{x^{b}}}\dot{x}^{b}\partial_{\dot{x}^{a}}+\left(\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}}\ddot{x}^{b}+\frac{\partial^{2}Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial{x^{b}}\partial{x^{c}}}\dot{x}^{b}\dot{x}^{c}\right)\partial_{\ddot{x}^{a}},\\
{\underline{e}_{k}}^{\langle{-1}\rangle}&=Q^{a}_{k}\partial_{\dot{x}^{a}}+{2}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}}\dot{x}^{b}\partial_{\ddot{x}^{a}},\\
{\underline{e}_{k}}^{\langle{-2}\rangle}&=2Q^{a}_{k}\,\partial_{\ddot{x}^{a}}.\end{cases}

(4.12)

(Note that
(Q^{a}_{k})^{(1)}=\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial{x^{b}}}\dot{x}^{b}
and

(Q^{a}_{k})^{(2)}=\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}}\ddot{x}^{b}+\frac{\partial^{2}Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial{x^{b}}\partial{x^{c}}}\dot{x}^{b}\dot{x}^{c}
. The above formulas for
{\underline{e}_{k}}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}
can also be obtained from (
3.18
) and (
3.29
).)
We check whether vector fields
{e_{k}}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}
and
{\underline{e}_{k}}^{\langle{{\alpha}}\rangle}
are
\sharp^{2}
-related, where
\sharp^{2}(x^{a},y^{i},z^{\mu})=(x^{a},\dot{x}^{a}=Q^{a}_{k}y^{k},\ddot{x}^{a}=Q^{a}_{\mu}z^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{a}_{ij}y^{i}y^{j})
. Straightforward calculations leads to the following system of equations (referred to as AL HA equations):

\displaystyle Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{\mu}_{k}=Q^{a}_{k}

(4.13a)

\displaystyle Q^{a}_{kj}+Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{\mu}_{jk}=2\,\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}}Q^{b}_{j}

(4.13b)

\displaystyle Q^{a}_{k}Q_{ij}^{k}=\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{ij}

(4.13c)

\displaystyle Q^{a}_{\nu}Q^{\nu}_{\mu i}+\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{\mu}}{\partial x^{b}}Q^{b}_{i}=\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}Q^{b}_{\mu}

(4.13d)

\displaystyle Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}+\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{ij}}{\partial x^{b}}Q^{b}_{k}+Q^{a}_{li}Q^{l}_{jk}+Q^{a}_{lj}Q^{l}_{ik}=\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}}Q^{b}_{ij}+2\,\frac{\partial^{2}Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}\partial x^{c}}Q^{b}_{i}Q^{c}_{j}

(4.13e)

where

\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{ij}:=Q^{b}_{i}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{j}}{\partial x^{b}}-Q^{b}_{j}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}},

(4.14)

(The equations (
4.13a
), (
4.13b
) correspond to the cases
{\alpha}=-2
and
{\alpha}=-1
, respectively; while (
4.13c
) (
4.13d
) and (
4.13e
) correspond to the case
{\alpha}=0
. Note also that the equations (
4.13a
), (
4.13b
), (
4.13c
), and (
4.13d
) follows immadiately from (

AL
∂

), (

AL
ψ

), (

AL
A

), and (

AL
□

), respectively.)
Note that

[\sharp e_{i},\sharp e_{j}]=\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{ij}\partial_{x^{a}}.

(4.15)

The equation (
4.13b
) can be replaced with

\displaystyle Q^{a}_{\mu}Q_{[ij]}=\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{ij},

(4.16a)

\displaystyle Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}+Q^{a}_{ij}=\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}

(4.16b)

where

\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}
is given in (
3.7
).

Completion of the proof of Theorem
3.16
.

We shall prove that if a skew HA
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
satisfy the conditions listed in Theorem
3.16
,
then it is almost Lie. It amounts to proving
that the vector fields
{e_{k}}^{[{\alpha}]}
and
{\underline{e}_{k}}^{[{\alpha}]}
, see (
3.18
) and (
4.12
), are
\sharp^{2}
-related for
{\alpha}=-2,-1,0
. We have already proved this for
{\alpha}=-2,-1
, so it remains to prove this for
{\alpha}=0
, i.e., to verify the equations (
4.13c
,
4.13d

4.13e
).

The equation (
4.13c
) means
\sharp[e_{i},e_{j}]=\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{ij}\partial_{x^{a}}
, which is true since the algebroid
(E^{1},\kappa^{1})
is AL. Next, the condition (
4.13d
) means
\sharp^{C}\Box_{e_{i}}c_{\mu}=[\sharp e_{i},\sharp^{C}c_{\mu}]
, and it follows
from (

AL
□

).

The proof of (
4.13e
) is a bit more involved.
We claim that

\sharp^{C}\circ{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}={\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}\circ\sharp^{\times 3}:A\times A\times A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M,

(4.17)

where
{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}
is given in (
3.23
) and
{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}
is the same structure map but associated with the HA
(\mathrm{T}^{2}M,\kappa^{2}_{M})
. Indeed,
\sharp^{C}\circ\omega=0
implies
\sharp^{C}\circ{\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}=0
and from
{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}={\omega}^{\mathrm{sym}}+\frac{1}{2}(\beta(s_{1},[s_{2},s])+\beta(s_{2},[s_{1},s]))
we find that

\sharp^{C}\circ{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{s}(s_{1},s_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\sharp^{C}\circ\left(\beta(s_{1},[s_{2},s])+\beta(s_{2},[s_{1},s])\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{i:AL_beta}}}{{=}}\sharp([[s,s_{1}],s_{2}]+[[s,s_{2}],s_{1}])=\left({\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}\right)_{\sharp s}(\sharp s_{1},\sharp s_{2}).

We shall show that (
4.17
)
gives (
4.13e
).
We shall work with an adapted coordinate system
(x^{a},y^{i},w^{\mu})
for
(E^{2},\kappa^{2})
(see Definition
3.7
), so
Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}=0
. The general idea is to express
{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{e_{k}}(e_{i},e_{j})
entirely in terms of the structure functions
Q^{a}_{i}
and its derivatives and then compare with
\left({\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{\mathrm{T}^{2}M}\right)_{\sharp e_{k}}(\sharp e_{i},\sharp e_{j})
,
which is easily seen to be of this form. From the expression for
\tilde{Q}^{\mu}_{ijk}
in (
3.21
)
we find that

{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{e_{k}}(e_{i},e_{j})=\frac{1}{2}\left(Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}-Q^{l}_{ik}Q^{\mu}_{lj}-Q^{l}_{jk}Q^{\mu}_{li}\right)c_{\mu},

hence

2\cdot\sharp^{C}\circ{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{e_{k}}(e_{i},e_{j})=Q^{a}_{\mu}\left(Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}-Q^{l}_{ik}Q^{\mu}_{lj}-Q^{l}_{jk}Q^{\mu}_{li}\right)\partial_{x^{a}}.

We replace
Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{l}_{jk}Q^{\mu}_{li}
with

Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{l}_{jk}Q^{\mu}_{li}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:Qa_kj}}}{{=}}Q^{l}_{jk}(-Q^{a}_{li}+2Q^{b}_{l}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e:QakQk_ij}}}{{=}}-Q^{l}_{jk}Q^{a}_{li}+2\widecheck{Q}^{b}_{jk}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}

and similarly for
Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{l}_{ik}Q^{\mu}_{lj}
and get

2\cdot\sharp^{C}\circ{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{e_{k}}(e_{i},e_{j})=(Q^{a}_{\mu}Q^{\mu}_{ij,k}+Q^{l}_{jk}Q^{a}_{li}+Q^{l}_{ik}Q^{a}_{lj}-2(\widecheck{Q}^{b}_{jk}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}+\widecheck{Q}^{b}_{ik}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{j}}{\partial x^{b}}))\partial_{x^{a}}

(4.18)

As
Q^{a}_{ij}=\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}
by (
4.16b
) the condition (
4.13e
) can be equivalently written as

2\cdot\sharp^{C}\circ{\delta}^{\mathrm{sym}}_{e_{k}}(e_{i},e_{j})=\left(2\frac{\partial^{2}Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}\partial x^{c}}Q^{b}_{i}Q^{c}_{j}+\widehat{Q}^{b}_{ji}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}}-Q^{b}_{k}\frac{\partial\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}}{\partial x^{b}}-2(\widecheck{Q}^{b}_{jk}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}+\widecheck{Q}^{b}_{ik}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{j}}{\partial x^{b}})\right)\partial_{x^{a}},

It remains to show that the last expressions coincides with
[\sharp e_{i},[\sharp e_{j},\sharp e_{k}]]+[\sharp e_{j},[\sharp e_{i},\sharp e_{k}]]
. This a direct calculation of the brackets of vector fields. Namely, from (
4.15
) we get

[\sharp e_{i},[\sharp e_{j},\sharp e_{k}]]=\left(Q^{b}_{i}\frac{\partial\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{jk}}{\partial x^{b}}-\widecheck{Q}^{b}_{jk}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}\right)\partial_{x^{a}}.

On the other hand, the following identity holds

2\frac{\partial^{2}Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}\partial x^{c}}Q^{b}_{i}Q^{c}_{j}+\widehat{Q}^{b}_{ji}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{k}}{\partial x^{b}}-Q^{b}_{k}\frac{\partial\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}}{\partial x^{b}}=Q^{b}_{i}\frac{\partial\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{jk}}{\partial x^{b}}+\widecheck{Q}^{b}_{jk}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{i}}{\partial x^{b}}+Q^{b}_{j}\frac{\partial\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{ik}}{\partial x^{b}}+\widecheck{Q}^{b}_{ik}\frac{\partial Q^{a}_{j}}{\partial x^{b}}

which can be verified by expanding
\widehat{Q}^{a}_{ij}
and
\widecheck{Q}^{a}_{jk}
using (
3.7
) and (
4.14
), and then grouping and cancelling similar terms.
On the LHS is the part of the expression (
4.18
) involving second derivatives. After plugging the RHS to (
4.18
) we shall easily recognize the desired formula.

∎

Lie HAs.

For completeness we provide a system of equations ensuring that a given AL HA is Lie.
They are obtained by examining the conditions listed in Theorem
3.20
on local frames
(e_{i})
and
(c_{\mu})
of the VBs
A
and
C
, respectively, see Remark
3.22
. The can be also obtained by examining the condition given in Remark
2.12
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\text{cyclic }i,j,k}Q_{ij}^{l}Q_{lk}^{m}=0,

(4.19a)

\displaystyle Q^{\mu}_{\nu k}Q^{\nu}_{i}=Q^{\mu}_{l}Q^{l}_{ik}+Q^{a}_{k}\frac{\partial Q^{\mu}_{i}}{\partial x^{a}},

(4.19b)

\displaystyle Q^{\mu}_{[ik]}=Q^{\mu}_{j}Q^{j}_{ik},

(4.19c)

\displaystyle Q^{\mu}_{{i^{\prime}}i,k}=Q^{j}_{ik}Q^{\mu}_{j{i^{\prime}}}-Q^{\nu}_{i{i^{\prime}}}Q^{\mu}_{\nu k}-Q^{j}_{k{i^{\prime}}}Q^{\mu}_{ij}+Q^{a}_{k}\frac{\partial Q^{\mu}_{i{i^{\prime}}}}{\partial x^{a}},

(4.19d)

\displaystyle Q^{j}_{k{k^{\prime}}}Q^{\mu}_{\nu j}+\frac{\partial Q^{j}_{k{k^{\prime}}}}{\partial x^{a}}Q^{a}_{\nu}Q^{\mu}_{j}=Q^{a}_{k}\frac{\partial Q^{\mu}_{\nu{k^{\prime}}}}{\partial x^{a}}+Q^{\rho}_{\nu k}Q^{\mu}_{\rho{k^{\prime}}}-Q^{a}_{{k^{\prime}}}\frac{\partial Q^{\mu}_{\nu k}}{\partial x^{a}}+Q^{\rho}_{\nu{k^{\prime}}}Q^{\mu}_{\rho k}

(4.19e)

The equation (
4.19a
) corresponds to the Jacobi identity, while (
4.19b
), (
4.19c
), (
4.19d
), and (
4.19e
) correspond to (

Lie
∂

), (

Lie
β

), (

Lie
ω

), and (

Lie
□

), respectively.

Proof of Conjecture
3.5
in the case
k=2
.

Let
(e_{i})
be a local basis of sections of
\sigma:A\rightarrow M
and denote
E_{i}^{[{\alpha}]}:=e_{i}^{\langle{\alpha}\rangle_{\kappa^{[2]}}}
– the
(A^{[2]},\kappa^{[2]})
–algebroid lifts,
\alpha=0,-1,2
.
Using (
3.18
) and Example
3.8
we find that

\begin{cases}E_{k}^{[0]}&=Q^{a}_{k}\partial_{x^{a}}+Q_{jk}^{i}y^{j}\partial_{y^{i}}+(Q^{m}_{nk}\,\dot{y}^{n}+\frac{1}{2}\,\widehat{Q}_{ij,k}^{m}\,y^{i}y^{j})\,\partial_{{\dot{y}}^{m}},\\
E_{k}^{[-1]}&=\partial_{y^{k}}+Q_{ik}^{m}\,y^{i}\,\partial_{{\dot{y}}^{m}},\\
E_{k}^{[-2]}&=2\partial_{{\dot{y}}^{k}}\end{cases}

(4.20)

It remains to show that the vector fields
E_{k}^{[\alpha]}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\alpha}(A^{[2]})
and
{e_{k}}^{\langle{\alpha}\rangle}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\alpha}(E^{2})
, given in (
3.18
), are
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}
-related. As
\mathrm{\Theta}^{1}
is the identity on
A
we only need to show that

(\mathrm{\Theta}^{2})^{\ast}{e_{k}}^{\langle{\alpha}\rangle}(z^{\mu})=E_{k}^{[\alpha]}((\mathrm{\Theta}^{2})^{\ast}z^{\mu})=E_{k}^{[\alpha]}(Q^{\mu}_{i}\dot{y}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\mu}_{(ij)}y^{i}y^{j})

(4.21)

for
\alpha=0,-1,-2
where
\mathrm{\Theta}^{2}:A^{[2]}\rightarrow E^{2}
is given in (
3.3
).
For
\alpha=-2
, this equation is satisfied automatically. For
\alpha=-1
, it results in equation (
4.19c
). For
\alpha=0
, the equation (
4.21
) can be expressed as a combination of (
4.19b
) and (
4.19d
).

∎

4.4
Representations up to homotopy of Lie algebroids

We shall review some calculus and sign conventions concerning representations up to homotopy of Lie algebroids. We follow the presentation given in
[
AC12
]
.

Let
(\sigma:A\rightarrow M,[\cdot,\cdot],\sharp)
be a Lie algebroid. Then
\Omega(A)=\operatorname{\Gamma}(\bigwedge^{\cdot}A^{\ast})
is known as
the algebra of
A
-differential forms
. In the case the tangent algebroid,
A=\mathrm{T}M
, it is simply the algebra of differential forms on the manifold
M
. There is an algebroid de Rham differential, called
A
-differential
d_{A}
, which is a derivation of
\Omega(A)
such that

(i)

d_{A}(f):s\mapsto(\sharp s)(f)
, for
s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)=\Omega^{0}(A)
;

(ii)

d_{A}(\omega):(s_{1},s_{2})\mapsto\omega([s_{1},s_{2}])-(\sharp s_{1})\left(\langle\omega,s_{2}\rangle\right)+(\sharp s_{2})\left(\langle\omega,s_{1}\rangle\right)
, where
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
,
\omega\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A^{\ast})=\Omega^{1}(A)
.

It is well known that a Lie algebroid can be equivalently described by means of
d_{A}
— a degree 1 derivation on
\Omega(A)
, see
[
Vai97
]
.

Let
F
be a vector bundle over the same base
M
. An
A
-connection on
F
is a mapping
\nabla:\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)\times\operatorname{\Gamma}(F)\rightarrow\operatorname{\Gamma}(F)
,
(s,v)\mapsto\nabla_{s}v
such that

\nabla_{fs}v=f\nabla_{s}v,\hskip 9.24994pt\nabla_{s}(fv)=f\nabla_{s}v+(\sharp s)(f)(v)

for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M),v\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(F),s\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
.
Recall that
the curvature
of an
A
-connection
\nabla
on
F
is the tensor given by

\mathrm{curv}_{\nabla}(s_{1},s_{2})(v)=\nabla_{s_{1}}\nabla_{s_{2}}v-\nabla_{s_{2}}\nabla_{s_{1}}v-\nabla_{[s_{1},s_{2}]}v,

(4.22)

where
s_{1},s_{2}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
,
v\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(F)
.

The space of
F
-valued
A
-differential forms
is defined as
\Omega(A;F)=\operatorname{\Gamma}(\bigwedge A^{\ast}\otimes F)
. In the setting of representations u.t.h., the vector bundle
F
is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, i.e.,
F=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}F^{i}
, where
F^{i}
is, so called, the vector bundle of
homogenous vectors of degree
i
. Given graded vector bundles
F
,
G
over the same manifold
M
, let
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G)=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{k}(F,G)
, where
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{k}(F,G)
denotes the bundle homomorphism from
F
to
G
that increase the degree by

k
. In other words, the fiber
\left(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{k}(F,G)\right)_{x}
over
x\in M
is a collection of linear maps
T_{i}:F^{i}_{x}\rightarrow G^{i+k}_{x}
. In the special case
F=G
we write
\underline{\operatorname{End}}(F)
for
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,F)
.
An element
\omega\in\Omega^{i}(A;F^{j})
is said to be of total degree
|\omega|=i+j
. There is an important operation, called

the wedge product

\Omega^{p}(A;E^{i})\otimes\Omega^{q}(A;F^{j})\to\Omega^{p+q}(A;G^{i+j}),\hskip 9.24994pt(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\alpha\wedge_{h}\beta,

associated with a degree preserving graded vector bundle morphism
h:E\otimes F\to G
. It is given by

(\alpha\wedge_{h}\beta)(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{p+q})=\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{qi}\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)h(\alpha(s_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,s_{\sigma_{p}}),\beta(s_{\sigma(k+1)},\ldots,s_{\sigma(p+q)})),

(4.23)

where the summation is over all
(p+q)
-shuffles,
s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{p+q}\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(A)
.
The left
\Omega(A)
-module structure on
\Omega(A;F)
is given by the wedge product associated with the
isomorphism
h_{L}:\mathbb{R}\otimes F\xrightarrow{\simeq}F
and is denoted by
\omega.\eta:=\omega\wedge_{h_{L}}\eta
.
On the other hand, the isomorphism
h_{R}:F\otimes\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{\simeq}F
gives rise to the right
\Omega(A)
-module structure on
\Omega(A;F)
,
\eta.\omega=\eta\wedge_{h_{R}}\omega
that makes
\Omega(A;F)
a symmetric
\Omega(A)
-bimodule,

\omega.\eta=(-1)^{|\omega||\eta|}\eta.\omega,

thanks to the sign
(-1)^{qi}
in (
4.23
).

We shall frequently encounter the case of the wedge product associated with the composition of homomorphisms
\circ:\operatorname{Hom}(G,H)\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(F,G)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(F,H)
which will be denoted by

\alpha\bm{\wedge}\beta:=\alpha\wedge_{\circ}\beta
. We have

(\alpha\bm{\wedge}\beta)\bm{\wedge}\gamma=\alpha\bm{\wedge}(\beta\bm{\wedge}\gamma).

(4.24)

for
\operatorname{Hom}
-valued
A
-forms
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
.
The operation
\bm{\wedge}
turns
\Omega(A;\underline{\operatorname{End}(F)})
into a graded associative algebra. The graded commutator on
\Omega(A;\underline{\operatorname{End}}(F))
is defined by
[\alpha,\beta]=\alpha\bm{\wedge}\beta-(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|}\beta\bm{\wedge}\alpha
.
Another case is the wedge product associated with the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}:\operatorname{Hom}(F,G)\otimes{F}\rightarrow G
which will be denoted in the same way as

\alpha\bm{\wedge}\eta:=\alpha\wedge_{\operatorname{ev}}\eta
where
\alpha\in\Omega(A,\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G))
,
\eta\in\Omega(A;F)
, since the evaluation map is a special case of the composition of maps, thanks to the isomorphism
F\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{R},F)
.

Definition 4.6
.

[
AC12
]
A
representation up to homotopy
of a Lie algebroid
A
consists
of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector bundle
F=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}F^{i}
and an operator, called
the structure operator
,

D:\Omega(A;F)\rightarrow\Omega(A;F)

of total degree one which satisfies
D\circ D=0
and the
graded derivation rule

D(\omega.\eta)=\mathrm{d}_{A}(\omega).\eta+(-1)^{k}\omega.D(\eta)

for
\omega\in\Omega^{k}(A)
,
\eta\in\Omega(A;F)
. A morphism
\Phi:(F,D_{F})\to(G,D_{G})
linking two representations u.t.h. is a degree zero
\Omega(A)
-module map
\Phi:\Omega(A;F)\to\Omega(A;G)
which commutes with the structure operators
D_{F}
and
D_{G}
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between
A
-forms
\omega\in\Omega(A;\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G))
of total degree
n
and operators
\widehat{\omega}:\Omega(A;F)\to\Omega(A;G)
of degree
n
which are
\Omega(A)
-linear in the graded sense. The operator
\widehat{\omega}
is given by
\widehat{\omega}(\eta)=\omega\bm{\wedge}\eta
. The equation (
4.24
) implies that
\widehat{\alpha\bm{\wedge}\beta}=\widehat{\alpha}\circ\widehat{\beta}
, where
\beta\in\Omega(A;\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G))
,
\alpha\in\Omega(A;\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(G,H))
.

A
cochain complex

(F,\partial)
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector bundle
F
equipped with an endomorphism
\partial\in\underline{\operatorname{End}}^{1}(F)
such that
\partial\circ\partial=0
, i.e., a differential on
F
. Such a differential can be consider as a
0
-form with values in
\underline{\operatorname{End}}(F)
, and gives rise to an operator
\widehat{\partial}:\Omega^{p}(A;F)\to\Omega^{p}(A,F)
,
\widehat{\partial}(\eta)=\partial\bm{\wedge}\eta
. It satisfies
\widehat{\partial}\circ\widehat{\partial}=0
and

\widehat{\partial}(\eta)(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{p})=(-1)^{p}\partial(\eta(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{p})),

The sign
(-1)^{p}
comes from (
4.23
). Given two complexes
(F,\partial^{F})
,
(G,\partial^{G})
and
\omega\in\Omega^{p}(A;\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{i}(F,G))
we get the induced
p
-form
\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\omega
defined as

\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\omega=\partial^{G}\bm{\wedge}\omega-(-1)^{|\omega|}\omega\bm{\wedge}\partial^{F}

which takes values in
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{i+1}(F,G)
, where
|\omega|=p+i
is the total degree of
\omega
. We have the complex
(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G),\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}})
with the differential
\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}
obtained by specializing to the case
p=0
which reads as

(\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}T)(v)=\partial^{G}(T(v))-(-1)^{|T|}T(\partial^{F}(v)),

where
v\in F
,
T\in\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G)
, and
|T|
stands for the degree of
T
.
It follows immediately from (
4.24
) that

\widehat{\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\omega}=\widehat{\partial^{G}}\circ\widehat{\omega}-(-1)^{|\omega|}\widehat{\omega}\circ\widehat{\partial^{F}}:\Omega(A;F)\rightarrow\Omega(A;G).

In the case
F=G
we can write
\widehat{\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\omega}=[\widehat{\partial^{F}},\widehat{\omega}]
.

Besides, an
A
-connection
\nabla
on a vector bundle
F

induces an operator
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}
on
\Omega(A;F)
of degree one defined by means of Koszul formula. This formula can be derived from
the conditions:

(i)

(\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\eta)(s)=\nabla_{s}\eta
for
0
-forms
\eta\in\Omega^{0}(A;F)=\operatorname{\Gamma}(F)
;

(ii)

the graded derivation rule:
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}(\omega.\eta)=\mathrm{d}_{A}(\omega).\eta+(-1)^{|\omega|}\omega.(\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\eta)
.

Given
A
-connections
\nabla^{F}
,
\nabla^{G}
on the graded vector bundles
F,G
, respectively, we get an
A
-connection
on
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G)
given by

(\nabla^{\operatorname{Hom}}T)(v)=\nabla^{G}(T(v))-T(\nabla^{F}v)

(4.25)

where
v\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(F)
, and
T
is a section of
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G)
. The corresponding operator
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}
on
\Omega(A;\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(F,G))
is given by

\widehat{\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\omega}=\mathrm{d}_{\nabla^{G}}\circ\widehat{\omega}{-}(-1)^{|\omega|}\widehat{\omega}\circ\mathrm{d}_{\nabla^{F}},\hskip 9.24994pt\omega\in\Omega(A;\operatorname{Hom}(F,G)).

To prove it one shows that
\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}

satisfies the graded derivation rule and that it reduces to the formula (
4.25
) when
T=\omega
is a
0
-form.

The structure operator
D
of a representation u.t.h. can be decomposed into a sequence of
\underline{\operatorname{End}}(F)
-valued
A
-forms and an
A
-connection giving an equivalent description. A precise statement is the following:

Proposition 4.7
.

[
AC12
, Proposition 3.2 and Definition 3.3]
The structure operator
D
on a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector bundle
F
can be equivalently given by a series of maps:

•

A degree
1
operator
\partial
on
F
making
(F,\partial)
a complex, i.e.,
\partial\circ\partial=0
.

•

An
A
-connection
\nabla^{F}
on
(F,\partial)
, i.e.,
\partial(\nabla^{F}v)=\nabla^{F}(\partial v)
for
v\in\operatorname{\Gamma}(F)
.

•

A 2-form
\omega_{2}\in\Omega^{2}(A;\underline{\operatorname{End}}^{-1}(F))
such that
\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\omega_{2}+\mathrm{curv}_{\nabla^{F}}=0
.

•

A sequence
(\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\ldots)
of
\underline{\operatorname{End}}(F)
-valued
A
-forms,
\omega_{i}\in\Omega^{i}(A;\underline{\operatorname{End}}^{1-i}(F))
15
15

15

Note that
\omega_{i}
has total degree 1
such that for each
n\geq 3

0=\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\omega_{n}+\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\omega_{n-1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-2}\omega_{i}\bm{\wedge}\omega_{n-i}\in\Omega^{n}(A;\underline{\operatorname{End}}^{2-n}(F)).

(4.26)

A morphism
\Phi
from
(F,D_{F})
to
(G,D_{G})
is given by a sequence of
A
-forms
\Phi_{i}\in\Omega^{i}(A;\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{-i}(F,G))
such that
\Phi_{0}
is a map of complexes and for each
n\geq 1

0=\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\Phi_{n}+\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\Phi_{n-1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-2}(\omega_{i}^{G}\bm{\wedge}\Phi_{n-i}-\Phi_{n-i}\bm{\wedge}\omega_{i}^{F})\ \in\Omega^{n}(A;\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}^{1-n}(F,G)).

(4.27)

Remark 4.8
.

Note that
\widehat{\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\omega_{i}}=\widehat{\partial^{F}}\circ\widehat{\omega_{i}}+\widehat{\omega_{i}}\circ\widehat{\partial^{F}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\omega_{i}}=\mathrm{d}_{\nabla^{F}}\circ\widehat{\omega_{i}}+\widehat{\omega_{i}}\circ\mathrm{d}_{\nabla^{F}}
(as
|\omega_{i}|=1
) and the structure equation (
4.26
) is equivalent to
D\circ D=0
where

D=\widehat{\partial^{F}}+\mathrm{d}_{\nabla^{F}}+\widehat{\omega_{2}}+\ldots:\Omega(A;F)\rightarrow\Omega(A;F).

(4.28)

Similarly, as
|\Phi|=0
, we have
\widehat{\partial^{\operatorname{Hom}}\Phi_{n}}=\widehat{\partial^{G}}\circ\widehat{\Phi}_{n}-\widehat{\Phi}_{n}\circ\widehat{\partial^{F}}
,
\widehat{\mathrm{d}_{\nabla}\Phi_{n-1}}=\mathrm{d}_{\nabla^{G}}\circ\widehat{\Phi}_{n-1}-\widehat{\Phi}_{n-1}\circ\mathrm{d}_{\nabla^{F}}
and the equation (
4.27
) means that the operator
\widehat{\Phi}=\widehat{\Phi_{0}}+\widehat{\Phi_{1}}+\ldots
intertwines the structure operators
D_{F}
and
D_{G}
.
